# Afghanistan

Country in South and Central Asia

For other uses, see [Afghanistan (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_\(disambiguation\) "Afghanistan (disambiguation)").

"Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan" redirects here. For the group, see [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban"). For the state that existed from 1996 to 2001, see [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\) "Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)").

**Afghanistan**,[\[e\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-37) officially the **Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan**,[\[f\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) is a landlocked country located at the crossroads of [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia "Central Asia") and [South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia "South Asia"). It is bordered by [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan") to the [east and south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durand_Line "Durand Line"),[\[g\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-40) [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran") to the [west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Iran_border "Afghanistan–Iran border"), [Turkmenistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmenistan "Turkmenistan") to the [northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Turkmenistan_border "Afghanistan–Turkmenistan border"), [Uzbekistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbekistan "Uzbekistan") to the [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Uzbekistan_border "Afghanistan–Uzbekistan border"), [Tajikistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajikistan "Tajikistan") to the [northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Tajikistan_border "Afghanistan–Tajikistan border"), and [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China") to the [northeast and east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93China_border "Afghanistan–China border"). Occupying 652,864 square kilometers (252,072 sq mi) of land, the country is predominantly mountainous with plains [in the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Turkestan "Afghan Turkestan") and [the southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistan_Basin "Sistan Basin"), which are separated by the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush "Hindu Kush") mountain range. [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul") is the country's capital and largest city. [Afghanistan's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Afghanistan "Demographics of Afghanistan") is estimated to be between 36–50 million.[\[d\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pop_note-31)

[Human habitation in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history_of_Afghanistan "Ancient history of Afghanistan") dates to the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic "Middle Paleolithic") era. Popularly referred to as the [graveyard of empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graveyard_of_empires "Graveyard of empires"),[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-41) the land [has witnessed numerous military campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasions_of_Afghanistan "Invasions of Afghanistan"), including those by the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_empire "Persian empire"), [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great"), the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire "Maurya Empire"), [Arab Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan "Muslim conquests of Afghanistan"), the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_the_Khwarazmian_Empire "Mongol invasion of the Khwarazmian Empire"), the [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_influence_in_Afghanistan#The_Great_Game "European influence in Afghanistan"), the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet–Afghan War"), and [a US-led coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%932021\) "War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)"). Afghanistan also served as the source from which the [Greco-Bactrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Bactrian_Kingdom "Greco-Bactrian Kingdom") and the [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire "Mughal Empire"), among others, rose to form major empires.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-42) Because of the various conquests and periods in both the [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Iran "Greater Iran") and [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India "Greater India") cultural spheres,[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43)[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44) the area was a center for [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism "Zoroastrianism"), Buddhism, Hinduism, and later Islam.[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-45) The modern state of Afghanistan began with the [Durrani Afghan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") in the 18th century,[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46) although [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan "Dost Mohammad Khan") is sometimes considered to be the founder of the first [modern Afghan state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Emirate of Afghanistan").[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-47) Afghanistan became a [buffer state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffer_state "Buffer state") in the [Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Game "Great Game") between the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire") and the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire "Russian Empire"). From India, the British attempted to subjugate Afghanistan but were repelled in the [First Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War "First Anglo-Afghan War"); the [Second Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Afghan_War "Second Anglo-Afghan War") saw a British victory. Following the [Third Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Anglo-Afghan_War "Third Anglo-Afghan War") in 1919, Afghanistan became free of foreign political hegemony, and emerged as the independent [Kingdom of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Afghanistan "Kingdom of Afghanistan") in 1926. This monarchy lasted almost half a century, until [Zahir Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Zahir_Shah "Mohammed Zahir Shah") was [overthrown in 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Afghan_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat "1973 Afghan coup d'état"), following which the [Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Afghanistan_\(1973%E2%80%931978\) "Republic of Afghanistan (1973–1978)") was established.

Since the late 1970s, Afghanistan's history has been dominated by extensive warfare, including [coups, invasions, insurgencies, and civil wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_conflict "Afghan conflict"). The conflict began in 1978 when [a communist revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution "Saur Revolution") established a [socialist state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan "Democratic Republic of Afghanistan") (itself a response to the dictatorship established following a [coup d'état in 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Afghan_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat "1973 Afghan coup d'état")), and subsequent infighting prompted the [Soviet Union to invade Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Afghanistan "Soviet invasion of Afghanistan") in 1979. [Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen "Mujahideen") fought against the Soviets in the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet–Afghan War") and continued fighting among themselves following the [Soviets' withdrawal in 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan "Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan"). The [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") controlled most of the country by 1996, but their [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\) "Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)") received little international recognition before its overthrow in the 2001 [US invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Afghanistan "United States invasion of Afghanistan"). The Taliban returned to power in 2021 after [capturing Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Kabul_\(2021\) "Fall of Kabul (2021)"), ending the [2001–2021 war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_\(2001%E2%80%932021\) "War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)").[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-48) As of June 2024[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit), the Taliban government remains [internationally unrecognized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Recognition of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan").

Afghanistan is rich in natural resources, including [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium"), iron, [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc "Zinc"), and copper. It is the second-largest producer of [cannabis resin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_resin "Cannabis resin"),[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49) and third largest of both [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron "Saffron")[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50) and [cashmere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cashmere_wool "Cashmere wool").[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-51) The country is a member of the [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation "South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation") and a founding member of the [Organization of Islamic Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Islamic_Cooperation "Organization of Islamic Cooperation"). Due to the effects of war in recent decades, the country has dealt with high levels of terrorism, [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Afghanistan "Poverty in Afghanistan"), and child malnutrition. Afghanistan remains among the world's [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_country "Least developed country"), ranking 182nd on the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index "Human Development Index"). Afghanistan's gross domestic product (GDP) is $81 billion by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity "Purchasing power parity") and $20.1 billion by nominal values. Per capita, its GDP is among the lowest of any country as of 2020[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit).

## Etymology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Etymology")]

Main article: [Name of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Afghanistan "Name of Afghanistan")

Some scholars suggest that the [root](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Root_\(linguistics\) "Root (linguistics)") name [*Afghān*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_\(ethnonym\) "Afghan (ethnonym)") is derived from the [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit") word *[Aśvakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C5%9Bvaka "Aśvaka")*, which was the name used for ancient inhabitants of the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush "Hindu Kush").[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-52) *Aśvakan* literally means "horsemen", "horse breeders", or "[cavalrymen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalry "Cavalry")" (from *[aśva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C5%9Bva "Aśva")*, the [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit") and [Avestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avestan "Avestan") words for "horse").[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-53)

Historically, the ethnonym *Afghān* was used to refer to ethnic [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns "Pashtuns").[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-54) The Arabic and Persian form of the name, *Afġān*, was first attested in the 10th-century geography book *[Hudud al-'Alam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudud_al-%27Alam "Hudud al-'Alam")*.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55) The last part of the name, "*[-stan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/-stan "-stan")*", is a Persian suffix meaning "place of". Therefore, "Afghanistan" translates to "land of the Afghans", or "land of the Pashtuns" in a historical sense. According to the third edition of the *[Encyclopedia of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclopedia_of_Islam "Encyclopedia of Islam")*:[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56)

> The name Afghanistan (Afghānistān, land of the Afghans / Pashtuns, *afāghina*, sing. *afghān*) can be traced to the early eighth/fourteenth century, when it designated the easternmost part of the [Kartid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartid "Kartid") realm. This name was later used for certain regions in the [Ṣafavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_Iran "Safavid Iran") and [Mughal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire "Mughal Empire") empires that were inhabited by Afghans. While based on a state-supporting elite of [Abdālī / Durrānī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani "Durrani") Afghans, the [Sadūzāʾī Durrānī polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") that came into being in 1160 / 1747 was not called Afghanistan in its own day. The name became a state designation only during the colonial intervention of the nineteenth century.

The term "Afghanistan" was officially used in 1855, when the British recognized [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan "Dost Mohammad Khan") as king of [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Emirate of Afghanistan").[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019317-57)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: History")]

Main article: [History of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Afghanistan "History of Afghanistan")

### Prehistory and antiquity

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Prehistory and antiquity")]

Main article: [Ancient history of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history_of_Afghanistan "Ancient history of Afghanistan")

See also: [Hindu and Buddhist heritage of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_and_Buddhist_heritage_of_Afghanistan "Hindu and Buddhist heritage of Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Nomads_in_Badghis_Province.jpg/220px-Nomads_in_Badghis_Province.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nomads_in_Badghis_Province.jpg)

Tents of Afghan [nomads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad "Nomad") in the northern [Badghis Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badghis_Province "Badghis Province"). Early peasant farming villages came into existence about 7,000 years ago.

Excavations of prehistoric sites suggest that humans were living in what is now Afghanistan at least 50,000 years ago, and that farming communities in the area were among the earliest in the world. An important site of early historical activities, many believe that Afghanistan compares to [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt "Egypt") in the historical value of its archaeological sites.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-JFS-58)[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-59) Artifacts typical of the [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic "Paleolithic"), [Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic "Mesolithic"), [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic "Neolithic"), [Bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age "Bronze Age"), and [Iron Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age "Iron Age") have been found in Afghanistan. Urban civilization is believed to have begun as early as 3000 BCE, and the early city of [Mundigak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mundigak "Mundigak") (near [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") in the south of the country) was a center of the [Helmand culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_culture "Helmand culture"). More recent findings established that the [Indus Valley Civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilization "Indus Valley Civilization") stretched up towards modern-day Afghanistan. An Indus Valley site has been found on the [Oxus River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxus_River "Oxus River") at [Shortugai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortugai "Shortugai") in northern Afghanistan.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60)[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-61)[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-62)

After 2000 BCE successive waves of semi-nomadic people from Central Asia began moving south into Afghanistan; among them were many [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages "Indo-European languages")-speaking [Indo-Iranians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Iranians "Indo-Iranians"). These tribes later migrated further into South Asia, Western Asia, and toward Europe via the area north of the [Caspian Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspian_Sea "Caspian Sea"). The region at the time was referred to as [Ariana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariana "Ariana").[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-JFS-58)[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-63) By the middle of the 6th century BCE, the Achaemenids overthrew the [Medes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medes "Medes") and incorporated [Arachosia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arachosia "Arachosia"), [Aria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aria_\(satrapy\) "Aria (satrapy)"), and [Bactria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria "Bactria") within its eastern boundaries. An [inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigraphy "Epigraphy") on the tombstone of [Darius I of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_I_of_Persia "Darius I of Persia") mentions the [Kabul Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabulistan "Kabulistan") in a list of the 29 countries that he had conquered.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64) The region of [Arachosia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arachosia "Arachosia"), around [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") in modern-day southern Afghanistan, used to be primarily Zoroastrian and played a key role in the transfer of the Avesta to [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persis "Persis") and is thus considered by some to be the "second homeland of Zoroastrianism".[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Gnoli-1989-65)[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Gnoli-1989a-66)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ARACHOSIA_%E2%80%93_Encyclopaedia_Iranica-67)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/ScythianBelt.jpg/220px-ScythianBelt.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ScythianBelt.jpg)

A "Bactrian gold" [Scythian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythian "Scythian") belt depicting [Dionysus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysus "Dionysus"), from [Tillya Tepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillya_Tepe "Tillya Tepe") in the ancient region of [Bactria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria "Bactria")

[Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great") and his Macedonian forces arrived in Afghanistan in 330 BCE after defeating [Darius III of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_III_of_Persia "Darius III of Persia") a year earlier in the [Battle of Gaugamela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gaugamela "Battle of Gaugamela"). Following Alexander's brief occupation, the successor state of the [Seleucid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid_Empire "Seleucid Empire") controlled the region until 305 BCE, when they gave much of it to the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire "Maurya Empire") as part of an alliance treaty. The Mauryans controlled the area south of the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush "Hindu Kush") until they were overthrown in about 185 BCE. Their decline began 60 years after [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka "Ashoka")'s rule ended, leading to the [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic "Hellenistic") reconquest by the [Greco-Bactrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Bactrians "Greco-Bactrians"). Much of it soon broke away and became part of the [Indo-Greek Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Greek_Kingdom "Indo-Greek Kingdom"). They were defeated and expelled by the [Indo-Scythians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Scythians "Indo-Scythians") in the late 2nd century BCE.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68)[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERunion200744-69) The [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road "Silk Road") appeared during the first century BCE, and Afghanistan flourished with trade, with routes to China, India, Persia, and north to the cities of [Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukhara "Bukhara"), [Samarkand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samarkand "Samarkand"), and [Khiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khiva "Khiva") in present-day Uzbekistan.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-70) Goods and ideas were exchanged at this center point, such as Chinese silk, Persian silver and Roman gold, while the region of present Afghanistan was mining and trading [lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli "Lapis lazuli") stones[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71) mainly from the [Badakhshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badakhshan "Badakhshan") region.

During the first century BCE, the [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire "Parthian Empire") subjugated the region but lost it to their [Indo-Parthian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Parthian "Indo-Parthian") vassals. In the mid-to-late first century CE the vast [Kushan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushan_Empire "Kushan Empire"), centered in Afghanistan, became great patrons of Buddhist culture, making [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") flourish throughout the region. The Kushans were overthrown by the [Sassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sassanids "Sassanids") in the 3rd century CE, though the [Indo-Sassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Sassanids "Indo-Sassanids") continued to rule at least parts of the region. They were followed by the [Kidarites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidarite "Kidarite") who, in turn, was replaced by the [Hephthalites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hephthalites "Hephthalites"). They were replaced by the [Turk Shahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turk_Shahi "Turk Shahi") in the 7th century. The Buddhist Turk Shahi of Kabul was replaced by a Hindu dynasty before the Saffarids conquered the area in 870, this Hindu dynasty was called [Hindu Shahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Shahi "Hindu Shahi").[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72) Much of the northeastern and southern areas of the country remained dominated by [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist "Buddhist") culture.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-73)[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-74)

### Medieval period

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Medieval period")]

Main articles: [Muslim conquests of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan "Muslim conquests of Afghanistan"), [Mongol invasion of Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Central_Asia "Mongol invasion of Central Asia"), and [Ghaznavid campaigns in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavid_campaigns_in_India "Ghaznavid campaigns in India")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Ghurid_Empire_according_to_Schwartzberg_Atlas%2C_p.147.png/220px-Ghurid_Empire_according_to_Schwartzberg_Atlas%2C_p.147.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ghurid_Empire_according_to_Schwartzberg_Atlas,_p.147.png)

The [Ghurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghurid_dynasty "Ghurid dynasty") originated from [Ghor Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghor_Province "Ghor Province") in central Afghanistan.

[Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab "Arab") [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim "Muslim") brought Islam to [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") and [Zaranj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaranj "Zaranj") in 642 CE and began spreading eastward; some of the native inhabitants they encountered accepted it while others revolted. Before the [arrival of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_conquest_of_Afghanistan "Islamic conquest of Afghanistan"), the region used to be home to various beliefs and cults, often resulting in [Syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism "Syncretism") between the dominant religions[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75)[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-76) such as [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism "Zoroastrianism"),[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Gnoli-1989-65)[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Gnoli-1989a-66)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ARACHOSIA_%E2%80%93_Encyclopaedia_Iranica-67) [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") or [Greco-Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Buddhism "Greco-Buddhism"), [Ancient Iranian religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Iranian_religion "Ancient Iranian religion"),[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Allen-77) [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism"), Christianity,[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78)[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79) and Judaism.[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80)[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81) An exemplification of the syncretism in the region would be that people were patrons of Buddhism but still worshipped local Iranian gods such as [Ahura Mazda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahura_Mazda "Ahura Mazda"), [Lady Nana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nana_\(Kushan_goddess\) "Nana (Kushan goddess)"), [Anahita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anahita "Anahita") or [Mihr (Mithra)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithra "Mithra") and portrayed [Greek gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology "Greek mythology") as protectors of Buddha.[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-82)[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Allen-77)[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-83) The [Zunbils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zunbils "Zunbils") and Kabul Shahi were first conquered in 870 CE by the [Saffarid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffarid_dynasty "Saffarid dynasty") Muslims of Zaranj. Later, the [Samanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanids "Samanids") extended their Islamic influence south of the Hindu Kush. The [Ghaznavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavids "Ghaznavids") rose to power in the 10th century.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-84)[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85)[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86)

By the 11th century, [Mahmud of Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_of_Ghazni "Mahmud of Ghazni") had defeated the remaining Hindu rulers and effectively [Islamized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamized "Islamized") the wider region,[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEwans200222%E2%80%9323-87) with the exception of [Kafiristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafiristan "Kafiristan").[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88) Mahmud made [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni "Ghazni") into an important city and patronized intellectuals such as the historian [Al-Biruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Biruni "Al-Biruni") and the poet [Ferdowsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdowsi "Ferdowsi").[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89) The [Ghaznavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaznavid_dynasty "Ghaznavid dynasty") was [overthrown by the Ghurids in 1186](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Lahore_\(1186\) "Siege of Lahore (1186)"), whose architectural achievements included the remote [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam "Minaret of Jam"). The Ghurids controlled Afghanistan for less than a century before being conquered by the [Khwarazmian dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwarazmian_dynasty "Khwarazmian dynasty") in 1215.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEwans200223-90)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Genghis_Khan_empire-en.svg/260px-Genghis_Khan_empire-en.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Genghis_Khan_empire-en.svg)

[Mongol invasions and conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasions_and_conquests "Mongol invasions and conquests")

In 1219 CE, [Genghis Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genghis_Khan "Genghis Khan") and his [Mongol army overran the region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_the_Khwarazmian_Empire "Mongol invasion of the Khwarazmian Empire"). His troops are said to have annihilated the Khwarazmian cities of [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") and [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh "Balkh") as well as [Bamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan,_Afghanistan "Bamyan, Afghanistan").[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91) The destruction caused by the Mongols forced many locals to return to an agrarian rural society.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92) Mongol rule continued with the [Ilkhanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilkhanate "Ilkhanate") in the northwest while the [Khalji dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalji_dynasty "Khalji dynasty") administered the Afghan tribal areas south of the Hindu Kush until the invasion of [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur "Timur") (aka Tamerlane), who established the [Timurid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire "Timurid Empire") in 1370. Under the rule of [Shah Rukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Rukh "Shah Rukh"), the city of Herat[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93) served as the focal point of the [Timurid Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Renaissance "Timurid Renaissance"), whose glory matched [Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florence "Florence") of the [Italian Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Renaissance "Italian Renaissance") as the center of a cultural rebirth.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94)[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95)

In the early 16th century [Babur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babur "Babur") arrived from [Ferghana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferghana "Ferghana") and captured Kabul from the [Arghun dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arghun_dynasty "Arghun dynasty").[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201292%E2%80%9393-96) [Babur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babur "Babur") would go on to conquer the Afghan [Lodi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lodi_dynasty "Lodi dynasty") who had ruled the Delhi Sultanate in the [First Battle of Panipat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Battle_of_Panipat "First Battle of Panipat").[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201275-97) Between the 16th and 18th century, the Uzbek [Khanate of Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Bukhara "Khanate of Bukhara"), Iranian [Safavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavids "Safavids"), and Indian [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughals "Mughals") ruled parts of the territory.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997319,_321-98) During the medieval period, the northwestern area of Afghanistan was referred to by the regional name [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Khorasan "Greater Khorasan"), which was commonly used up to the 19th century among natives to describe their country.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99)[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100)[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101)[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102)

### Hotak dynasty

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Hotak dynasty")]

Main article: [Hotak dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotak_dynasty "Hotak dynasty")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Mapofthehotaks1728.png/260px-Mapofthehotaks1728.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mapofthehotaks1728.png)

Map of the Hotak Empire at its height in 1728, disputed between [Hussain Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Hotak "Hussain Hotak") (centered in Kandahar) and [Ashraf Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashraf_Hotak "Ashraf Hotak") (centered in Isfahan)

In 1709, [Mirwais Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwais_Hotak "Mirwais Hotak"), a local [Ghilzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghilzai "Ghilzai") tribal leader, successfully rebelled against the [Safavids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty "Safavid dynasty"). He defeated [Gurgin Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurgin_Khan "Gurgin Khan"), the Georgian governor of Kandahar under the Safavids, and established his own kingdom.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Browne-103) Mirwais died in 1715, and was succeeded by his brother [Abdul Aziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Aziz_Hotak "Abdul Aziz Hotak"), who was soon killed by Mirwais's son [Mahmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Hotak "Mahmud Hotak") for possibly planning to sign a peace with the Safavids. Mahmud led the Afghan army in 1722 to the Persian capital of [Isfahan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan "Isfahan"), and captured the city after the [Battle of Gulnabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Gulnabad "Battle of Gulnabad") and proclaimed himself King of Persia.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Browne-103) The Afghan dynasty was ousted from Persia by [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah "Nader Shah") after the 1729 [Battle of Damghan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Damghan_\(1729\) "Battle of Damghan (1729)").

In 1738, [Nader Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah "Nader Shah") and his [forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_dynasty "Afsharid dynasty") captured Kandahar in the [siege of Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Kandahar "Siege of Kandahar"), the last Hotak stronghold, from Shah [Hussain Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussain_Hotak "Hussain Hotak"). Soon after, the Persian and Afghan forces [invaded India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nader_Shah%27s_invasion_of_India "Nader Shah's invasion of India"), Nader Shah had plundered Delhi, alongside his 16-year-old commander, [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani "Ahmad Shah Durrani") who had assisted him on these campaigns. Nader Shah was assassinated in 1747.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-105)

### Durrani Empire

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Durrani Empire")]

Main articles: [Durrani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") and [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani "Ahmad Shah Durrani")

After the death of Nader Shah in 1747, [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani "Ahmad Shah Durrani") had returned to Kandahar with a contingent of 4,000 [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns "Pashtuns"). The Abdalis had "unanimously accepted" Ahmad Shah as their new leader. With his ascension in 1747, Ahmad Shah had led multiple campaigns against the [Mughal empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_empire "Mughal empire"), [Maratha empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_empire "Maratha empire"), and then-receding [Afsharid empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran "Afsharid Iran"). Ahmad Shah had captured [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul") and [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar") from the Mughal appointed governor, Nasir Khan. Ahmad Shah had then conquered [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") in 1750, and had also captured [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir "Kashmir") in 1752.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106) Ahmad Shah had launched two campaigns into [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Khorasan "Greater Khorasan"), 1750–1751 and 1754–1755.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107) His first campaign had seen the siege of [Mashhad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashhad "Mashhad"), however, he was forced to retreat after four months. In November 1750, he moved to siege [Nishapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Nishapur "Khanate of Nishapur"), but he was unable to capture the city and was forced to retreat in early 1751. [Ahmad Shah returned in 1754](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Campaign_to_Khorasan_\(1754-55\) "Durrani Campaign to Khorasan (1754-55)"); he captured [Tun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdows "Ferdows"), and on 23 July, he sieged [Mashhad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashhad "Mashhad") once again. Mashhad had fallen on 2 December, but [Shahrokh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahrokh_Shah "Shahrokh Shah") was [reappointed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afsharid_Iran "Afsharid Iran") in 1755. He was forced to give up [Torshiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torshiz "Torshiz"), [Bakharz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakharz "Bakharz"), [Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torbat-e_Jam "Torbat-e Jam"), [Khaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaf,_Iran "Khaf, Iran"), and [Turbat-e Haidari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torbat-e_Heydarieh "Torbat-e Heydarieh") to the Afghans, as well as accept Afghan sovereignty. Following this, Ahmad Shah sieged [Nishapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Nishapur "Khanate of Nishapur") once again, and captured it.

Ahmad Shah [invaded India eight times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_campaign_of_Ahmad_Shah_Durrani "Indian campaign of Ahmad Shah Durrani") during his reign,[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehta248-108) beginning in 1748. Crossing the Indus River, his armies sacked and absorbed [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore") into the [Durrani Realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire"). He met Mughal armies at the [Battle of Manupur (1748)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manupur_\(1748\) "Battle of Manupur (1748)"), where he was defeated and forced to retreat back to Afghanistan.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-109) He returned the next year in 1749 and captured the area around [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore") and [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab "Punjab"), presenting it as an Afghan victory for this campaign.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019122%E2%80%93123-110) From 1749 to 1767, Ahmad Shah led six more invasions, the most important being the last; the [Third Battle of Panipat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Battle_of_Panipat "Third Battle of Panipat") created a power vacuum in northern India, halting [Maratha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_Empire "Maratha Empire") expansion.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Portrait_of_Ahmad-Shah_Durrani._Mughal_miniature._ca._1757%2C_Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France.jpg/220px-Portrait_of_Ahmad-Shah_Durrani._Mughal_miniature._ca._1757%2C_Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Portrait_of_Ahmad-Shah_Durrani._Mughal_miniature._ca._1757,_Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France.jpg)

Portrait of [Ahmad Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Durrani "Ahmad Shah Durrani") c. 1757

Ahmad Shah Durrani died in October 1772, and a civil war over succession followed, with his named successor, [Timur Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur_Shah_Durrani "Timur Shah Durrani") succeeding him after the defeat of his brother, Suleiman Mirza.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019149-111) Timur Shah Durrani ascended to the throne in November 1772, having defeated a coalition under Shah Wali Khan and Humayun Mirza. Timur Shah began his reign by consolidating power toward himself and people loyal to him, purging Durrani Sardars and influential tribal leaders in [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul") and [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar"). One of Timur Shah's reforms was to move the capital of the [Durrani Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") from [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") to [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul"). Timur Shah fought multiple series of rebellions to consolidate the empire, and he also led campaigns into [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab "Punjab") against the Sikhs like his father, though more successfully. The most prominent example of his battles during this campaign was when he led his forces under Zangi Khan Durrani – with over 18,000 men total of Afghan, Qizilbash, and Mongol cavalrymen – against over 60,000 Sikh men. The Sikhs lost over 30,000 in this battle and staged a Durrani resurgence in the [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab "Punjab") region[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112) The Durranis lost [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan "Multan") in 1772 after Ahmad Shah's death. Following this victory, Timur Shah was able to lay siege to [Multan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multan "Multan") and recapture it,[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113) incorporating it into the Durrani Empire once again, reintegrating it as a province until the [Siege of Multan (1818)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Multan_\(1818\) "Siege of Multan (1818)"). Timur Shah was succeeded by his son [Zaman Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaman_Shah_Durrani "Zaman Shah Durrani") after his death in May 1793. Timur Shah's reign oversaw the attempted stabilization and consolidation of the empire. However, Timur Shah had over 24 sons, which plunged the empire in civil war over succession crises.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019155-114)

[Zaman Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaman_Shah_Durrani "Zaman Shah Durrani") succeeded to the [Durrani Throne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") following the death of his father, Timur Shah Durrani. His brothers [Mahmud Shah Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Shah_Durrani "Mahmud Shah Durrani") and Humayun Mirza revolted against him, with Humayun centered in [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") and Mahmud Shah centered in [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat").[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019158-115) Zaman Shah would defeat Humayun and force the loyalty of Mahmud Shah Durrani.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019158-115) Securing his position on the throne, Zaman Shah led three campaigns into [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab "Punjab"). The first two campaigns captured [Lahore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore "Lahore"), but he retreated due to intel about a possible [Qajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_Iran "Qajar Iran") invasion. Zaman Shah embarked on his third campaign for [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab "Punjab") in 1800 to deal with a rebellious Ranjit Singh.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019162-116) However, he was forced to withdraw, and Zaman Shah's reign was ended by Mahmud Shah Durrani.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019162-116) However, just under two years into his reign, Mahmud Shah Durrani was deposed by his brother [Shah Shuja Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Shuja_Durrani "Shah Shuja Durrani") on 13 July 1803.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019166-117) Shah Shuja attempted to consolidate the [Durrani Realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire "Durrani Empire") but was deposed by his brother at the [Battle of Nimla (1809)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nimla_\(1809\) "Battle of Nimla (1809)").[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019172-118) Mahmud Shah Durrani defeated Shah Shuja and forced him to flee, usurping the throne again. His second reign began on 3 May 1809.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019176-119)

### Barakzai dynasty and British wars

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Barakzai dynasty and British wars")]

Further information: [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan "Dost Mohammad Khan"), [Herat (1793–1863)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_\(1793%E2%80%931863\) "Herat (1793–1863)"), [Maimana Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimana_Khanate "Maimana Khanate"), [Principality of Qandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar "Principality of Qandahar"), [European influence in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_influence_in_Afghanistan "European influence in Afghanistan"), [Anglo-Afghan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Afghan_Wars "Anglo-Afghan Wars"), [Durand Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durand_Line "Durand Line"), and [Afghan Civil War (1928–1929)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1928%E2%80%931929\) "Afghan Civil War (1928–1929)")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Map_of_Afghanistan_March_1839.png/300px-Map_of_Afghanistan_March_1839.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map_of_Afghanistan_March_1839.png)

Map of Afghanistan ([Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Emirate of Afghanistan")) and surrounding nations in 1839, during the [First Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War "First Anglo-Afghan War"). [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan "Dost Mohammad Khan")'s realm can be seen as the Emirate of Kabul, with the [Principality of Qandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar "Principality of Qandahar") and the [Emirate of Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Herat "Emirate of Herat") seen as well.

By the early 19th century, the Afghan empire was under threat from the [Persians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty "Qajar dynasty") in the west and the [Sikh Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire "Sikh Empire") in the east. [Fateh Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fateh_Khan_Barakzai "Fateh Khan Barakzai"), leader of the [Barakzai tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barakzai_dynasty "Barakzai dynasty"), installed many of his brothers in positions of power throughout the empire. Fateh Khan was [brutally murdered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_and_Execution_of_Fateh_Khan_Barakzai "Torture and Execution of Fateh Khan Barakzai") in 1818 by [Mahmud Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Shah_Durrani "Mahmud Shah Durrani"). As a result, the brothers of Fateh Khan and the [Barakzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barakzai "Barakzai") tribe rebelled, and a civil war brewed. During this turbulent period, Afghanistan fractured into many states, including the [Principality of Qandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar "Principality of Qandahar"), [Emirate of Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_\(1793%E2%80%931863\) "Herat (1793–1863)"), [Khanate of Qunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz_Khanate "Kunduz Khanate"), [Maimana Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimana_Khanate "Maimana Khanate"), and numerous other warring polities. The most prominent state was the [Emirate of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Emirate of Afghanistan"), ruled by [Dost Mohammad Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad_Khan "Dost Mohammad Khan").[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-120)[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121)

With the collapse of the Durrani Empire, and the exile of the [Sadozai Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_dynasty "Durrani dynasty") to be left to rule in [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_\(1793%E2%80%931863\) "Herat (1793–1863)"), Punjab and Kashmir were lost to [Ranjit Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranjit_Singh "Ranjit Singh"), ruler of the [Sikh Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire "Sikh Empire"), who invaded [Khyber Pakhtunkhwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pakhtunkhwa "Khyber Pakhtunkhwa") in March 1823 and captured the city of [Peshawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar "Peshawar") following the [Battle of Nowshera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Nowshera "Battle of Nowshera"). In 1834, Dost Mohammad Khan led numerous campaigns, firstly [campaigning to Jalalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dost_Mohammad%27s_Campaign_to_Jalalabad_\(1834\) "Dost Mohammad's Campaign to Jalalabad (1834)"), and then allying with his rival brothers in [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar "Principality of Qandahar") to defeat [Shah Shuja Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Shuja_Durrani "Shah Shuja Durrani") and the British in the [Expedition of Shuja ul-Mulk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedition_of_Shuja_ul-Mulk "Expedition of Shuja ul-Mulk").[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019205-122) In 1837, Dost Mohammad Khan attempted to conquer Peshawar and sent a large force under his son [Wazir Akbar Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_Akbar_Khan "Wazir Akbar Khan"), leading to the [Battle of Jamrud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Jamrud "Battle of Jamrud"). [Akbar Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_Akbar_Khan "Wazir Akbar Khan") and the Afghan army failed to capture the [Jamrud Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamrud_Fort "Jamrud Fort") from the [Sikh Khalsa Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Khalsa_Army "Sikh Khalsa Army"), but killed Sikh Commander [Hari Singh Nalwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_Singh_Nalwa "Hari Singh Nalwa"), thus ending the [Afghan-Sikh Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan-Sikh_Wars "Afghan-Sikh Wars"). By this time the British were advancing from the east, capitalizing on the decline of the Sikh Empire after it had its own period of turbulence following the death of [Ranjit Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranjit_Singh "Ranjit Singh"), which engaged the [Emirate of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Emirate of Afghanistan") in the [first major conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War "First Anglo-Afghan War") during "[The Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Game "The Great Game")".[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-123)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Afghan_foot_soldiers_in_1841.jpg/220px-Afghan_foot_soldiers_in_1841.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghan_foot_soldiers_in_1841.jpg)

[Afghan tribesmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_tribes "Pashtun tribes") in 1841, painted by British officer [James Rattray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Rattray "James Rattray")

In 1839 a [British](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Army "British Army") expeditionary force marched into Afghanistan, invading the [Principality of Qandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Qandahar "Principality of Qandahar"), and in August 1839, seized [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul"). Dost Mohammad Khan defeated the British in the [Parwan campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parwan_Campaign_\(1840\) "Parwan Campaign (1840)"), but surrendered following his victory. He was replaced with the former Durrani ruler [Shah Shuja Durrani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Shuja_Durrani "Shah Shuja Durrani") as the new [ruler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_Afghanistan "List of heads of state of Afghanistan") of [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul"), a de facto puppet of the British.[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124)[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-125) Following an uprising that saw the assassination of [Shah Shuja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Shuja_Durrani "Shah Shuja Durrani"), the [1842 retreat from Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1842_retreat_from_Kabul "1842 retreat from Kabul") of British-Indian forces and the [annihilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1842_retreat_from_Kabul "1842 retreat from Kabul") of [Elphinstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_George_Keith_Elphinstone "William George Keith Elphinstone")'s army, and the punitive expedition of [The Battle of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kabul_\(1842\) "Battle of Kabul (1842)") that led to its sacking, the British gave up on their attempts to try to subjugate Afghanistan, allowing Dost Mohammad Khan to return as ruler. Following this, Dost Mohammad pursued a myriad of campaigns to unite most of Afghanistan in his reign, launching numerous incursions including against the surrounding states such as the [Hazarajat campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazarajat_Campaign_of_1843 "Hazarajat Campaign of 1843"), [conquest of Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Conquest_of_Balkh "Afghan Conquest of Balkh"), [conquest of Kunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Conquest_of_Kunduz "Afghan Conquest of Kunduz"), and the [conquest of Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_Kandahar "Conquest of Kandahar"). Dost Mohammad led his final campaign against [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_\(1793%E2%80%931863\) "Herat (1793–1863)"), conquering it and re-uniting Afghanistan. During his campaigns of re-unification, he held friendly relations with the British despite the First Anglo-Afghan War, and affirmed their status in the [Second Anglo-Afghan treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Afghan_Treaty_\(1857\) "Second Anglo-Afghan Treaty (1857)") of 1857, while [Bukhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Bukhara "Emirate of Bukhara") and internal religious leaders pressured Dost Mohammad to invade India during the [Indian Rebellion of 1857](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857 "Indian Rebellion of 1857").[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-126)

Dost Mohammad died in June 1863, a few weeks after his successful [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_campaign_of_1862%E2%80%931863 "Herat campaign of 1862–1863") to Herat. Following his death, a civil war ensued among his sons, prominently [Mohammad Afzal Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Afzal_Khan "Mohammad Afzal Khan"), [Mohammad Azam Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Azam_Khan "Mohammad Azam Khan"), and [Sher Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sher_Ali_Khan "Sher Ali Khan"). Sher Ali won the resulting [Afghan Civil War (1863–1869)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1863%E2%80%931869\) "Afghan Civil War (1863–1869)") and ruled Afghanistan until his death in 1879. In his final years, the British returned to Afghanistan in the [Second Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Afghan_War "Second Anglo-Afghan War") to fight perceived Russian influence in the region. Sher Ali retreated to northern Afghanistan, intending to create a resistance there similar to his predecessors, Dost Mohammad Khan, and Wazir Akbar Khan. His untimely death however, saw [Yaqub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Yaqub_Khan "Mohammad Yaqub Khan") declared the new Amir, leading to Britain gaining control of Afghanistan's foreign relations as part of the [Treaty of Gandamak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Gandamak "Treaty of Gandamak") of 1879, making it an official [British Protected State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Protectorate "British Protectorate").[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019359-366-127)[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-128) [An uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_the_British_Residency_in_Kabul "Siege of the British Residency in Kabul") however, re-started the conflict, and Yaqub Khan was deposed. During this tumultuous period, [Abdur Rahman Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdur_Rahman_Khan "Abdur Rahman Khan") began his rise to power, becoming an eligible candidate to become Amir after he seized [much of Northern Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Anglo-Afghan_War#Exploits_of_Abdur_Rahman_Khan "Second Anglo-Afghan War"). Abdur Rahman marched on [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul"), and was declared Amir, being recognized by the British as well. Another uprising by [Ayub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_Khan_\(Emir_of_Afghanistan\) "Ayub Khan (Emir of Afghanistan)") threatened the British, where rebels confronted and defeated British forces in the [Battle of Maiwand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Maiwand "Battle of Maiwand"). Following up on his victory, Ayub Khan unsuccessfully [besieged Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kandahar_\(1880\) "Battle of Kandahar (1880)"), and his decisive defeat saw the end of the Second Anglo-Afghan War, with Abdur Rahman secured firmly as Amir.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2019376-383-129) In 1893, Abdur Rahman signed an agreement in which the ethnic [Pashtun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun "Pashtun") and [Baloch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baloch_people "Baloch people") territories were divided by the [Durand Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durand_Line "Durand Line"), which forms the modern-day border between Pakistan and Afghanistan. [Shia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Islam_in_Afghanistan "Shia Islam in Afghanistan")-dominated [Hazarajat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazarajat "Hazarajat") and pagan [Kafiristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafiristan "Kafiristan") remained politically independent until being [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquests_of_Afghanistan "Muslim conquests of Afghanistan") by Abdur Rahman Khan in 1891–1896. He was known as the "Iron Amir" for his features and his ruthless methods against tribes.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-130) He died in 1901, succeeded by his son, [Habibullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habibullah_Khan "Habibullah Khan").

> How can a small power like Afghanistan, which is like a goat between these lions \[Britain and Russia] or a grain of wheat between two strong millstones of the grinding mill, stand in the midway of the stones without being ground to dust?
>
> — [Abdur Rahman Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdur_Rahman_Khan "Abdur Rahman Khan"), the "Iron Amir", in 1900[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-131)[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-132)

During the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War "First World War"), when Afghanistan was neutral, Habibullah Khan was met by officials of the central powers in the [Niedermayer–Hentig Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niedermayer%E2%80%93Hentig_Expedition "Niedermayer–Hentig Expedition"). They called on Afghanistan to declare full independence from the United Kingdom, join them and attack British India, as part of the [Hindu–German Conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu%E2%80%93German_Conspiracy "Hindu–German Conspiracy"). The effort to bring Afghanistan into the Central Powers failed, but it sparked discontent among the population about maintaining neutrality with the British. Habibullah was assassinated in February 1919, and [Amanullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_Khan "Amanullah Khan") eventually assumed power. A staunch supporter of the 1915–1916 expeditions, Amanullah Khan invaded British India, beginning the [Third Anglo-Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Anglo-Afghan_War "Third Anglo-Afghan War"), and entering British India via the [Khyber Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khyber_Pass "Khyber Pass").[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-133)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/King_Amanullah_standing.jpg/170px-King_Amanullah_standing.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:King_Amanullah_standing.jpg)

[Amanullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_Khan "Amanullah Khan") proclaimed himself [King of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Afghanistan "King of Afghanistan") in June 1926.

After the end of the Third Anglo-Afghan War and the signing of the [Treaty of Rawalpindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Afghan_Treaty_of_1919 "Anglo-Afghan Treaty of 1919") on 19 August 1919, [Emir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir_of_Afghanistan "Emir of Afghanistan") Amanullah Khan declared the [Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Emirate of Afghanistan") a [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_state "Sovereign state") and fully [independent state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_state "Independent state"). He moved to end his country's traditional isolation by establishing diplomatic relations with the international community, particularly with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") and the [Weimar Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weimar_Republic "Weimar Republic").[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134)[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135) He proclaimed himself [King of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Afghanistan "King of Afghanistan") on 9 June 1926, forming the [Kingdom of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Afghanistan "Kingdom of Afghanistan"). He introduced several reforms intended to modernize his nation. A key force behind these reforms was [Mahmud Tarzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_Tarzi "Mahmud Tarzi"), an ardent supporter of the education of women. He fought for Article 68 of Afghanistan's 1923 [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Afghanistan "Constitution of Afghanistan"), which made elementary education compulsory. [Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery "Slavery") was abolished in 1923.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136) King Amanullah's wife, [Queen Soraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soraya_Tarzi "Soraya Tarzi"), was an important figure during this period in the fight for woman's education and against their oppression.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-137)

Some of the reforms, such as the abolition of the traditional [burqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burqa "Burqa") for women and the opening of co-educational schools, alienated many tribal and religious leaders, leading to the [Afghan Civil War (1928–1929)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1928%E2%80%931929\) "Afghan Civil War (1928–1929)"). King Amanullah abdicated in January 1929, and soon after Kabul fell to [Saqqawist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saqqawist "Saqqawist") forces led by [Habibullah Kalakani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habibullah_Kalakani "Habibullah Kalakani").[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-138) [Mohammed Nadir Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Nadir_Shah "Mohammed Nadir Shah"), Amanullah's cousin, defeated and killed Kalakani in October 1929, and was declared King Nadir Shah.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139) He abandoned the reforms of King Amanullah in favor of a more gradual approach to modernization, but was assassinated in 1933 by [Abdul Khaliq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Khaliq_Hazara_\(assassin\) "Abdul Khaliq Hazara (assassin)").[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-140)

Mohammed Zahir Shah succeeded to the throne and reigned as king from 1933 to 1973. During the [tribal revolts of 1944–1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_tribal_revolts_of_1944%E2%80%931947 "Afghan tribal revolts of 1944–1947"), King Zahir's reign was challenged by [Zadran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadran_\(Pashtun_tribe\) "Zadran (Pashtun tribe)"), [Safi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safi_\(Pashtun_tribe\) "Safi (Pashtun tribe)"), [Mangal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangal_\(Pashtun_tribe\) "Mangal (Pashtun tribe)"), and [Wazir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wazir_\(Pashtun_tribe\) "Wazir (Pashtun tribe)") tribesmen led by [Mazrak Zadran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazrak_Zadran "Mazrak Zadran"), [Salemai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salemai "Salemai"), and [Mirzali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faqir_Ipi "Faqir Ipi"), among others – many of whom were [Amanullah loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_loyalist "Amanullah loyalist"). Afghanistan joined the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations "League of Nations") in 1934. The 1930s saw the development of roads, infrastructure, the founding of a [national bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank "Da Afghanistan Bank"), and increased education. Road links in the north played a large part in a growing cotton and textile industry.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Eur2002-141) The country built close relationships with the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers "Axis powers"), with [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") having the largest share in Afghan development at the time.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-142)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Zahir_Shah_of_Afghanistan_in_1930s-cropped.jpg/170px-Zahir_Shah_of_Afghanistan_in_1930s-cropped.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Zahir_Shah_of_Afghanistan_in_1930s-cropped.jpg)

[King Zahir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Zahir_Shah "Mohammed Zahir Shah"), the last reigning monarch of Afghanistan, who reigned from 1933 until 1973

Until 1946 King Zahir ruled with the assistance of his uncle, who held the post of [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Afghanistan "Prime Minister of Afghanistan") and continued the policies of Nadir Shah. Another uncle, [Shah Mahmud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_Mahmud_Khan "Shah Mahmud Khan"), became prime minister in 1946 and experimented with allowing greater political freedom. He was replaced in 1953 by [Mohammed Daoud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Daoud_Khan "Mohammed Daoud Khan"), a [Pashtun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun "Pashtun") nationalist who sought the creation of a [Pashtunistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtunistan "Pashtunistan"), leading to highly tense relations with Pakistan.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-143) Daoud Khan pressed for social modernization reforms and sought a closer relationship with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"). Afterward, the [1964 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Constitution_of_Afghanistan "1964 Constitution of Afghanistan") was formed, and the first non-royal prime minister was sworn in.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Eur2002-141)

Zahir Shah, like his father Nadir Shah, had a policy of maintaining national independence while pursuing gradual modernization, creating nationalist feeling, and improving relations with the United Kingdom. Afghanistan was neither a participant in [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") nor aligned with either power bloc in the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War"). However, it was a beneficiary of the latter rivalry as both the Soviet Union and the United States vied for influence by building Afghanistan's main highways, airports, and other vital infrastructure. On a per capita basis, Afghanistan received more Soviet [development aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_aid "Development aid") than any other country. In 1973, while the King was in Italy, Daoud Khan launched a [bloodless coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Afghan_coup "1973 Afghan coup") and became the first [president of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Afghanistan "President of Afghanistan"), abolishing the monarchy.

### Democratic Republic and Soviet war

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: Democratic Republic and Soviet war")]

Main articles: [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan "Democratic Republic of Afghanistan"), [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet–Afghan War"), and [Afghan Civil War (1989–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989%E2%80%931992\) "Afghan Civil War (1989–1992)")

Further information: [History of Afghanistan (1978–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Afghanistan_\(1978%E2%80%931992\) "History of Afghanistan (1978–1992)")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Afgan1987_Gardez_UAZ469.jpg/220px-Afgan1987_Gardez_UAZ469.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afgan1987_Gardez_UAZ469.jpg)

Soviet troops in [Gardez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gardez "Gardez"), Afghanistan in 1987

In April 1978, the communist [People's Democratic Party of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan "People's Democratic Party of Afghanistan") (PDPA) seized power in a bloody coup d'état against then-President [Mohammed Daoud Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Daoud_Khan "Mohammed Daoud Khan"), in what is called the [Saur Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution "Saur Revolution"). The PDPA declared the establishment of the [Democratic Republic of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_Afghanistan "Democratic Republic of Afghanistan"), with its first leader named as [People's Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan "People's Democratic Party of Afghanistan") General Secretary [Nur Muhammad Taraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nur_Muhammad_Taraki "Nur Muhammad Taraki").[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEwans2002186%E2%80%9388-144) This would trigger a series of events that would dramatically turn Afghanistan from a poor and secluded (albeit peaceful) country to a hotbed of international terrorism.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-145) The PDPA initiated various social, symbolic, and land distribution reforms that provoked strong opposition, while also brutally oppressing political dissidents. This caused unrest and quickly expanded into a state of [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_conflict "Afghan conflict") by 1979, waged by guerrilla *[mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen "Mujahideen")* (and smaller [Maoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maoist "Maoist") guerrillas) against regime forces countrywide. It quickly turned into a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war "Proxy war") as the Pakistani government provided these rebels with covert training centers, the United States [supported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone "Operation Cyclone") them through Pakistan's [Inter-Services Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Intelligence "Inter-Services Intelligence") (ISI),[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-146) and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") sent thousands of military advisers to support the PDPA regime.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-147) Meanwhile, there was increasingly hostile friction between the competing factions of the PDPA – the dominant [Khalq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalq "Khalq") and the more moderate [Parcham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parcham "Parcham").[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-148)

In October 1979, PDPA General Secretary Taraki was assassinated in an internal coup orchestrated by then-prime minister [Hafizullah Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafizullah_Amin "Hafizullah Amin"), who became the new general secretary of the [People's Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan "People's Democratic Party of Afghanistan"). The situation in the country deteriorated under Amin, and thousands of people went missing.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-149) Displeased with Amin's government, the [Soviet Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Army "Soviet Army") invaded the country in December 1979, heading for Kabul and killing Amin.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield2012234-150) A Soviet-organized regime, led by Parcham's [Babrak Karmal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babrak_Karmal "Babrak Karmal") but inclusive of both factions (Parcham and Khalq), filled the vacuum. Soviet troops in more substantial numbers were deployed to stabilize Afghanistan under Karmal, marking the beginning of the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet–Afghan War").[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-151) Lasting nine years, the war caused the deaths of between 562,000[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-152) and 2 million Afghans,[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-153)[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154)[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-155)[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-156)[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-157)[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-158)[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159)\[*[excessive citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources#Bundling_citations "Wikipedia:Citing sources")*] and displaced about 6 million people who subsequently fled Afghanistan, mainly to [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Pakistan "Afghans in Pakistan") and [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Iran "Afghans in Iran").[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-160) Heavy air bombardment destroyed many countryside villages, millions of [landmines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmine "Landmine") were planted,[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-161) and some cities such as [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") and [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") were also damaged from bombardment. After the [Soviet withdrawal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan "Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan"), the [civil war ensued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989-92\) "Afghan Civil War (1989-92)") until the communist regime under People's Democratic Party leader [Mohammad Najibullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Najibullah "Mohammad Najibullah") collapsed in 1992.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-162)[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-163)[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield2012239,_244-164)

The Soviet–Afghan War had drastic social effects on Afghanistan. The militarization of society led to heavily armed police, private bodyguards, openly armed civil defense groups, and other such things becoming the norm in Afghanistan for decades thereafter.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-165) The traditional power structure had shifted from clergy, community elders, intelligentsia, and military in favor of powerful [warlords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warlords "Warlords").[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-166)

### Post–Cold War conflict

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Post–Cold War conflict")]

See also: [Afghan Civil War (1992–1996)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1992%E2%80%931996\) "Afghan Civil War (1992–1996)"), [Afghan Civil War (1996–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1996%E2%80%932001\) "Afghan Civil War (1996–2001)"), [Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan_\(1996%E2%80%932001\) "Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996–2001)"), and [Northern Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Alliance "Northern Alliance")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/War_in_Afghanistan_%281992%E2%80%932001%29.png/330px-War_in_Afghanistan_%281992%E2%80%932001%29.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:War_in_Afghanistan_\(1992%E2%80%932001\).png)

Development of the civil war from 1992 to late 2001

Another civil war broke out after the [creation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peshawar_Accords "Peshawar Accords") of a dysfunctional coalition [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Afghanistan "Islamic State of Afghanistan") between leaders of various *mujahideen* factions. Amid a state of anarchy and factional infighting,[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-167)[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-2005-168)[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-169) various *mujahideen* factions committed widespread rape, murder and extortion,[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-2005-168)[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-170)[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-1998-171) while Kabul was heavily bombarded and partially destroyed by the fighting.[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-1998-171) Several failed reconciliations and alliances occurred between different leaders.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-172) The [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") emerged in September 1994 as a movement and
