### Foreign relations

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Foreign relations")]

Main article: [Foreign relations of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Afghanistan "Foreign relations of Afghanistan")

See also: [Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Afghanistan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_\(Afghanistan\) "Ministry of Foreign Affairs (Afghanistan)")

Afghanistan became a member of the United Nations in 1946.[\[325\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997642-332) Historically, Afghanistan had strong relations with Germany, one of the first countries to recognize Afghanistan's independence in 1919; the Soviet Union, which provided much aid and military training for Afghanistan's forces and includes the signing of a Treaty of Friendship in 1921 and 1978; and [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93India_relations "Afghanistan–India relations"), with which a friendship treaty was signed in 1950.[\[326\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-333) Relations with [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Pakistan_relations "Afghanistan–Pakistan relations") have often been tense for various reasons such as the [Durand Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durand_Line "Durand Line") border issue and alleged Pakistani involvement in Afghan insurgent groups.

The present Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan is currently internationally [unrecognized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Recognition of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan"), but has had notable unofficial ties with [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93China_relations "Afghanistan–China relations"), Pakistan, and Qatar.[\[327\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-334)[\[328\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-335) Under the previous Islamic Republic of Afghanistan, it enjoyed cordial relations with a number of [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO "NATO") and allied nations, particularly the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93United_States_relations "Afghanistan–United States relations"), [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Canada_relations "Afghanistan–Canada relations"), [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations "Afghanistan–United Kingdom relations"), [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Germany_relations "Afghanistan–Germany relations"), Australia, and [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Turkey_relations "Afghanistan–Turkey relations"). In 2012, the United States and the then-republic in Afghanistan signed their [Strategic Partnership Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%E2%80%93Afghanistan_Strategic_Partnership_Agreement "US–Afghanistan Strategic Partnership Agreement") in which Afghanistan became a [major non-NATO ally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_non-NATO_ally "Major non-NATO ally").[\[329\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-336) Such qualification was rescinded by US President [Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden "Joe Biden") in July 2022.[\[330\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-337)

### Military

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=19 "Edit section: Military")]

Main article: [Afghan Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Armed_Forces "Afghan Armed Forces")

The [Armed Forces of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Armed Forces of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan") captured a large amount of weapons, hardware, vehicles, aerocrafts, and equipment from the [Afghan National Security Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Security_Forces "Afghan National Security Forces") following the [2021 Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive "2021 Taliban offensive") and the [Fall of Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Kabul_\(2021\) "Fall of Kabul (2021)"). The total value of the captured equipment has been estimated at US$83 billion.[\[331\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-338)[\[332\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-339)

### Human rights

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Human rights")]

See also: [Human rights in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Afghanistan "Human rights in Afghanistan"), [Women in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Afghanistan "Women in Afghanistan"), [LGBT rights in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Afghanistan "LGBT rights in Afghanistan"), [Treatment of women by the Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treatment_of_women_by_the_Taliban "Treatment of women by the Taliban"), and [Afghan refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_refugees "Afghan refugees")

Homosexuality is [taboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taboo "Taboo") in Afghan society;[\[333\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-340) according to the Penal Code, homosexual intimacy is punished by up to a year in prison.[\[334\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-341) Under [Sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law "Sharia law") offenders can be [punished by death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_penalty_for_homosexuality "Death penalty for homosexuality").[\[335\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-342)[\[336\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-343) However, an ancient tradition involving male homosexual acts between children and older men (typically wealthy warlords or elite people) called *[bacha bazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacha_bazi "Bacha bazi")* persists.

Religious minorities such as Sikhs,[\[337\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-344) Hindus,[\[338\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-345) and Christians have reportedly faced persecution.[\[339\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-346)[\[340\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-347)

Since May 2022, all women in Afghanistan have been required by law to wear full-body coverings when in public (either a [burqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burqa "Burqa") or an [abaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abaya "Abaya") paired with a [niqāb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niq%C4%81b "Niqāb"), which leaves only the eyes uncovered).[\[341\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-348)[\[342\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-349) First [Deputy Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_the_Islamic_Emirate_of_Afghanistan "Deputy Leader of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan") [Sirajuddin Haqqani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirajuddin_Haqqani "Sirajuddin Haqqani") claimed the decree is only advisory and no form of [hijab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijab "Hijab") is compulsory in Afghanistan,[\[343\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-350) though this contradicts the reality.[\[344\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-351) It has been speculated that there is a genuine internal policy division over women's rights between hardliners, including leader Hibatullah Akhundzada, and pragmatists, though they publicly present a united front.[\[345\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-352) Another decree was issued shortly after the first, requiring female TV presenters to cover their faces during broadcasts.[\[346\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-353) Since the Taliban takeover, suicides among women have become more common, and the country could now be one of the few where the rate of suicide among women surpasses that among men.[\[347\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-354)[\[348\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-355)[\[349\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-356)

In May 2022, the Taliban dissolved Afghanistan's Human Rights Commission along with four other government departments, citing the country's budget deficit.[\[350\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-357)

## Economy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Economy")]

Main article: [Economy of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Afghanistan "Economy of Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Afghan_pomegranate_processing.jpg/220px-Afghan_pomegranate_processing.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghan_pomegranate_processing.jpg)

Workers processing [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate_production_in_Afghanistan "Pomegranate production in Afghanistan") (*anaar*), for which Afghanistan is famous in Asia

Afghanistan's nominal GDP was $20.1 billion in 2020, or $81 billion by [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity "Purchasing power parity") (PPP).[\[351\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-imf2-358) Its [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita "List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita") is $2,459 (PPP) and $611 by nominal.[\[351\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-imf2-358) Despite having $1 trillion or more in mineral deposits,[\[352\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-359) it remains one of the world's [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries "Least developed countries"). Afghanistan's rough physical geography and its landlocked status has been cited as reasons why the country has always been among the least developed in the modern era – a factor where progress is also slowed by contemporary conflict and political instability.[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fisher-2002-272) The country imports over $7 billion worth of goods but exports only $784 million, mainly fruits and [nuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nut_\(fruit\) "Nut (fruit)"). It has $2.8 billion in [external debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt "External debt").[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275) The service sector contributed the most to the GDP (55.9%) followed by agriculture (23%) and industry (21.1%).[\[353\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-360)

[Da Afghanistan Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank "Da Afghanistan Bank") serves as the central bank of the nation[\[354\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-361) and the [Afghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_afghani "Afghan afghani") (AFN) is the national currency, with an exchange rate of about 75 Afghanis to 1 US dollar.[\[355\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-362) A number of local and foreign banks operate in the country, including the [Afghanistan International Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_International_Bank "Afghanistan International Bank"), [New Kabul Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Kabul_Bank "New Kabul Bank"), [Azizi Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azizi_Bank "Azizi Bank"), [Pashtany Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtany_Bank "Pashtany Bank"), [Standard Chartered Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chartered_Bank "Standard Chartered Bank"), and the [First Micro Finance Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_MicroFinance_Bank-Afghanistan "First MicroFinance Bank-Afghanistan").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Afghan_carpets_being_sold.jpg/300px-Afghan_carpets_being_sold.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghan_carpets_being_sold.jpg)

[Afghan rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_rugs "Afghan rugs") are one of Afghanistan's main exports.

One of the main drivers for the current economic recovery is the return of over 5 million [expatriates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_diaspora "Afghan diaspora"), who brought with them entrepreneurship and wealth-creating skills as well as much needed funds to start up businesses. Many Afghans are now involved in construction, which is one of the largest industries in the country.[\[356\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-363) Some of the major national construction projects include the $35 billion New Kabul City next to the capital, the Aino Mena project in Kandahar, and the [Ghazi Amanullah Khan Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Amanullah_Khan_Town "Ghazi Amanullah Khan Town") near Jalalabad.[\[357\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-364)[\[358\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-365)[\[359\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-366) Similar development projects have also begun in [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat"), [Mazar-e-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-e-Sharif "Mazar-e-Sharif"), and other cities.[\[360\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-367) An estimated 400,000 people enter the labor market each year.[\[361\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-368)

Several small companies and factories began operating in different parts of the country, which not only provide revenues to the government but also create new jobs. Improvements to the business environment have resulted in more than $1.5 billion in [telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications "Telecommunications") investment and created more than 100,000 jobs since 2003.[\[362\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-369) [Afghan rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_rug "Afghan rug") are becoming popular again, allowing many carpet dealers around the country to hire more workers; in 2016–17 it was the fourth most exported group of items.[\[363\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-370)

Afghanistan is a member of [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO "WTO"), [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAARC "SAARC"), [ECO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Cooperation_Organization "Economic Cooperation Organization"), and [OIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OIC "OIC"). It holds an observer status in [SCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shanghai_Cooperation_Organisation "Shanghai Cooperation Organisation"). In 2018, a majority of imports come from either Iran, China, Pakistan and Kazakhstan, while 84% of exports are to Pakistan and India.[\[364\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-371)

Since the Taliban's takeover of the country in August 2021, the United States has [frozen about $9 billion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_frozen_assets "Afghan frozen assets") in assets belonging to the [Afghan central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Da_Afghanistan_Bank "Da Afghanistan Bank"),[\[365\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-372) blocking the Taliban from accessing billions of dollars held in US bank accounts.[\[366\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-373)[\[367\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-374)

The [GDP of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Afghanistan "Economy of Afghanistan") is estimated to have dropped by 20% following the Taliban return to power. Following this, after months of free-fall, the Afghan economy began stabilizing, as a result of the Taliban's restrictions on smuggled imports, limits on banking transactions, and UN aid. In 2023, the Afghan economy began seeing signs of revival. This has also been followed by stable exchange rates, low inflation, stable revenue collection, and the rise of trade in exports.[\[368\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-375) In the third quarter of 2023, the [Afghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_afghani "Afghan afghani") rose to be the best performing currency in the world, climbing over 9% against the [US dollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_dollar "United States dollar").[\[369\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-376)

### Agriculture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Agriculture")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Afghan_Saffron.jpg/220px-Afghan_Saffron.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghan_Saffron.jpg)

Afghan [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron "Saffron")

Agricultural production is the backbone of Afghanistan's economy[\[370\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-377) and has traditionally dominated the economy, employing about 40% of the workforce as of 2018.[\[371\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-378) The country is known for producing [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate_production_in_Afghanistan "Pomegranate production in Afghanistan"), grapes, apricots, melons, and several other fresh and dry fruits. Afghanistan also became the world's top producer of [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis "Cannabis") in 2010.[\[372\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-379) In March 2023, however, cannabis production was banned by a decree from Hibatullah Akhundzada.[\[373\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-380)

[Saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron "Saffron"), the most expensive spice, grows in Afghanistan, particularly [Herat Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_Province "Herat Province"). In recent years, there has been an uptick in saffron production, which authorities and farmers are using to try to replace poppy cultivation. Between 2012 and 2019, the saffron cultivated and produced in Afghanistan was consecutively ranked the world's best by the International Taste and Quality Institute.[\[374\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-381)[\[375\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-382) Production hit record high in 2019 (19,469 kg of saffron), and one kilogram is sold domestically between $634 and $1147.[\[376\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-383)

The availability of cheap diesel-powered water pumps imported from China and Pakistan, and in the 2010s, of cheap solar power to pump water, resulted in expansion of agriculture and population in the southwestern deserts of Afghanistan in [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_Province "Kandahar Province"), [Helmand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_Province "Helmand Province") and [Nimruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimruz_Province "Nimruz Province") provinces in the 2010s. Wells have gradually been deepened, but water resources are limited. Opium is the major crop, but as of 2022, was under attack by the new Taliban government which, to suppress opium production, was systematically suppressing water pumping.[\[377\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-384)[\[378\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-385) In a 2023 report, poppy cultivation in southern Afghanistan was reduced by over 80% as a result of Taliban campaigns to stop its use toward opium. This included a 99% reduction of opium growth in the [Helmand Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmand_Province "Helmand Province").[\[379\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-386) In November 2023, a U.N report showed that in the entirety of Afghanistan, poppy cultivation dropped by over 95%, removing it from its place as being the world's largest opium producer.[\[380\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-387)[\[381\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-388)

### Mining

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=23 "Edit section: Mining")]

Main article: [Mining in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Afghanistan "Mining in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Lapis_Lazuli_from_Afghanistan.jpg/220px-Lapis_Lazuli_from_Afghanistan.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lapis_Lazuli_from_Afghanistan.jpg)

[Lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli "Lapis lazuli") stones

The country's natural resources include: coal, copper, iron ore, [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium"), [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium"), [rare earth elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare_earth_element "Rare earth element"), [chromite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromite "Chromite"), gold, [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc "Zinc"), [talc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talc "Talc"), [barite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barite "Barite"), [sulfur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur "Sulfur"), lead, [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble "Marble"), precious and [semi-precious stones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-precious_stones "Semi-precious stones"), natural gas, and petroleum.[\[382\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-peters2007-389)[\[383\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-bgs-390) In 2010, US and Afghan government officials estimated that untapped mineral deposits located in 2007 by the [US Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Geological_Survey "US Geological Survey") are worth at least $1 trillion.[\[384\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-bbcminerals-391)

[Michael E. O'Hanlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_E._O%27Hanlon "Michael E. O'Hanlon") of the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution "Brookings Institution") estimated that if Afghanistan generates about $10 billion per year from its [mineral deposits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_in_Afghanistan "Mining in Afghanistan"), its [gross national product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_national_product "Gross national product") would double and provide long-term funding for critical needs.[\[385\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-392) The [United States Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Geological_Survey "United States Geological Survey") (USGS) estimated in 2006 that northern Afghanistan has an average 460 million m3 (2.9 billion bbl) of [crude oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crude_oil "Crude oil"), 440 billion m3 (15.7 trillion cu ft) of natural gas, and 67 billion L (562 million US bbl) of [natural gas liquids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas_liquids "Natural gas liquids").[\[386\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-393) In 2011, Afghanistan signed an oil exploration contract with [China National Petroleum Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_National_Petroleum_Corporation "China National Petroleum Corporation") (CNPC) for the development of three oil fields along the Amu Darya river in the north.[\[387\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-394)

The country has significant amounts of [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium "Lithium"), copper, gold, coal, iron ore, and other [minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minerals "Minerals").[\[382\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-peters2007-389)[\[383\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-bgs-390)[\[388\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-395) The [Khanashin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanashin "Khanashin") [carbonatite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbonatite "Carbonatite") in Helmand Province contains 1,000,000 tonnes (980,000 [long tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_ton "Long ton"); 1,100,000 [short tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_ton "Short ton")) of [rare earth elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rare_earth_element "Rare earth element").[\[389\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-396) In 2007, a 30-year lease was granted for the [Aynak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mes_Aynak#Copper_Mine "Mes Aynak") copper mine to the [China Metallurgical Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Metallurgical_Group "China Metallurgical Group") for $3 billion,[\[390\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-397) making it the biggest foreign investment and private business venture in Afghanistan's history.[\[391\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-398) The state-run [Steel Authority of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_Authority_of_India "Steel Authority of India") won the mining rights to develop the huge [Hajigak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajigak_Pass "Hajigak Pass") iron ore deposit in central Afghanistan.[\[392\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-399) Government officials estimate that 30% of the country's untapped mineral deposits are worth at least $1 trillion.[\[384\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-bbcminerals-391) One official asserted that "this will become the backbone of the Afghan economy" and a Pentagon memo stated that Afghanistan could become the "Saudi Arabia of lithium".[\[393\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-400) The lithium reserves of 21 Mio. tons could amount to the ones of [Bolivia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivia "Bolivia"), which is currently viewed as the country with the largest lithium reserves.[\[394\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Hosp-2021-401) Other larger deposits are the ones of [bauxite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauxite "Bauxite") and [cobalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobalt "Cobalt").[\[394\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Hosp-2021-401)

Access to [biocapacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocapacity "Biocapacity") in Afghanistan is lower than world average. In 2016, Afghanistan had 0.43 global hectares[\[395\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-footprintdata-402) of biocapacity per person within its territory, much less than the world average of 1.6 global hectares per person.[\[396\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-403) In 2016 Afghanistan used 0.73 global hectares of biocapacity per person—their [ecological footprint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_footprint "Ecological footprint") of consumption. This means they use just under double as much biocapacity as Afghanistan contains. As a result, Afghanistan is running a biocapacity deficit.[\[395\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-footprintdata-402)

In September 2023, the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") signed mining contracts worth $6.5 billion, with extractions based on gold, iron, lead, and zinc in the provinces of Herat, Ghor, Logar, and Takhar.[\[397\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-404)

### Energy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=24 "Edit section: Energy")]

Main articles: [Energy in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Afghanistan "Energy in Afghanistan") and [Renewable energy in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_Afghanistan "Renewable energy in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Afghanistan_electricity_production.svg/220px-Afghanistan_electricity_production.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghanistan_electricity_production.svg)

Afghanistan electricity supply (1980–2019)

According to the [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank "World Bank"), 98% of the rural population have access to electricity in 2018, up from 28% in 2008.[\[398\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-405) Overall the figure stands at 98.7%.[\[399\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-406) As of 2016, Afghanistan produces 1,400 [megawatts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megawatt "Megawatt") of power, but still imports the majority of electricity via transmission lines from Iran and the Central Asian states.[\[400\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-407) The majority of electricity production is via [hydropower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydropower "Hydropower"), helped by the amount of rivers and streams that flow from the mountains.[\[401\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-408) However electricity is not always reliable and blackouts happen, including in Kabul.[\[402\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Afghanistan_Analysts_Network_%E2%80%93_English-2015-409) In recent years an increasing number of [solar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_power "Solar power"), [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass "Biomass") and wind power plants have been constructed.[\[403\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-410) Currently under development are the [CASA-1000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CASA-1000 "CASA-1000") project which will transmit electricity from Kyrgyzstan and Tajikistan, and the [Turkmenistan-Afghanistan-Pakistan-India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmenistan-Afghanistan-Pakistan-India_pipeline "Turkmenistan-Afghanistan-Pakistan-India pipeline") (TAPI) gas pipeline.[\[402\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Afghanistan_Analysts_Network_%E2%80%93_English-2015-409) Power is managed by the [Da Afghanistan Breshna Sherkat](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Da_Afghanistan_Breshna_Sherkat\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Da Afghanistan Breshna Sherkat (page does not exist)") (DABS, Afghanistan Electricity Company).

Important dams include the [Kajaki Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kajaki_Dam "Kajaki Dam"), [Dahla Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahla_Dam "Dahla Dam"), and the [Sardeh Band Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardeh_Band_Dam "Sardeh Band Dam").[\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-afghanistans.com-1-277)

### Tourism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Tourism")]

Main article: [Tourism in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Afghanistan "Tourism in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Contrasts_%284292970991%29.jpg/220px-Contrasts_%284292970991%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Contrasts_\(4292970991\).jpg)

[Band-e Amir National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band-e_Amir_National_Park "Band-e Amir National Park")

Tourism is a small industry in Afghanistan due to security issues. Nevertheless, some 20,000 foreign tourists visit the country annually as of 2016.[\[404\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-411) In particular an important region for domestic and international tourism is the picturesque [Bamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan "Bamyan") Valley, which includes lakes, canyons and historical sites, helped by the fact it is in a safe area away from insurgent activity.[\[405\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-412)[\[406\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-413) Smaller numbers visit and trek in regions such as the [Wakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wakhan "Wakhan") Valley, which is also one of the world's most remote communities.[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-414) From the late 1960s onwards, Afghanistan was a popular stop on the famous [hippie trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippie_trail "Hippie trail"), attracting many Europeans and Americans. Coming from Iran, the trail traveled through various Afghan provinces and cities including [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat"), [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") and [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul") before crossing to northern Pakistan, northern India, and [Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal "Nepal").[\[408\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-415)[\[409\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-416) Tourism peaked in 1977, the year before the start of political instability and armed conflict.[\[410\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-417)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Jam_afghanistan_ghorprovince_islamic_architecture.jpg/170px-Jam_afghanistan_ghorprovince_islamic_architecture.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jam_afghanistan_ghorprovince_islamic_architecture.jpg)

The [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam "Minaret of Jam"), a UNESCO [World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site "World Heritage Site")

The city of [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni "Ghazni") has significant history and historical sites, and together with [Bamyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan "Bamyan") city have in recent years been voted Islamic Cultural Capital and South Asia Cultural Capital respectively.[\[411\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-418) The cities of [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat"), [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar"), [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh "Balkh"), and [Zaranj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaranj "Zaranj") are also very historic. The [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam "Minaret of Jam") in the [Hari River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_River,_Afghanistan "Hari River, Afghanistan") valley is a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_World_Heritage_Site "UNESCO World Heritage Site"). A cloak reputedly worn by Islam's prophet [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad "Muhammad") is kept in the [Shrine of the Cloak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_the_Cloak "Shrine of the Cloak") in Kandahar, a city founded by [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great") and the first capital of Afghanistan. The [citadel of Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citadel_of_Alexander "Citadel of Alexander") in the western city of Herat has been renovated in recent years and is a popular attraction. In the north of the country is the [Shrine of Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_Ali "Shrine of Ali"), believed by many to be the location where [Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali "Ali") was buried.[\[412\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997115-419) The [National Museum of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Afghanistan "National Museum of Afghanistan") in Kabul hosts a large number of Buddhist, [Bactrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bactria "Bactria") Greek and early Islamic antiquities; the museum suffered greatly by civil war but has been slowly restoring since the early 2000s.[\[413\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-420)

Unexpectedly, tourism has seen improvement in Afghanistan following the Taliban takeover. Active efforts by the Taliban encouraged tourism to increase from 691 tourists in 2021, to 2,300 in 2022, to 5,200 in 2023, with some estimates of between 7,000 and 10,000.[\[414\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-421)[\[415\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-422)[\[416\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-423) This is, however, threatened by the [Islamic State – Khorasan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_Khorasan_Province "Islamic State – Khorasan Province"), who took responsibility for attacks on tourists, such as the [2024 Bamyan shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Bamyan_shooting "2024 Bamyan shooting").[\[417\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-424)

### Communication

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Communication")]

Main article: [Communications in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communications_in_Afghanistan "Communications in Afghanistan")

Telecommunication services in Afghanistan are provided by [Afghan Telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Telecom "Afghan Telecom"), [Afghan Wireless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Wireless "Afghan Wireless"), [Etisalat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etisalat "Etisalat"), [MTN Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTN_Group "MTN Group"), and [Roshan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roshan_\(telco\) "Roshan (telco)"). The country uses its own space [satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite "Satellite") called [Afghansat 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghansat_1 "Afghansat 1"), which provides services to millions of phone, internet, and television subscribers. By 2001 following years of civil war, telecommunications was virtually a non-existent sector, but by 2016 it had grown to a $2 billion industry, with 22 million mobile phone subscribers and 5 million internet users. The sector employs at least 120,000 people nationwide.[\[418\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-425)

### Transportation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Transportation")]

Main article: [Transport in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Afghanistan "Transport in Afghanistan")

See also: [List of airports in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Afghanistan "List of airports in Afghanistan") and [Rail transport in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Afghanistan "Rail transport in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Fran%C3%A7oise_Foliot_-_Afghanistan_043.jpg/220px-Fran%C3%A7oise_Foliot_-_Afghanistan_043.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fran%C3%A7oise_Foliot_-_Afghanistan_043.jpg)

The [Salang Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salang_Tunnel "Salang Tunnel")

Due to Afghanistan's geography, transport between various parts of the country has historically been difficult. The backbone of Afghanistan's road network is [Highway 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highway_1_\(Afghanistan\) "Highway 1 (Afghanistan)"), often called the "Ring Road", which extends for 2,210 kilometres (1,370 mi) and connects five major cities: Kabul, Ghazni, Kandahar, Herat and Mazar-i-Sharif,[\[419\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-426) with spurs to Kunduz and Jalalabad and various border crossings, while skirting around the mountains of the Hindu Kush.[\[420\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-427)

The Ring Road is crucially important for domestic and international trade and the economy.[\[421\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-428) A key portion of the Ring Road is the [Salang Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salang_Tunnel "Salang Tunnel"), completed in 1964, which facilitates travel through the Hindu Kush mountain range and connects northern and southern Afghanistan.[\[422\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-429) It is the only land route that connects Central Asia to the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent "Indian subcontinent").[\[423\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-430) Several mountain passes allow travel between the Hindu Kush in other areas. Serious traffic accidents are common on Afghan roads and highways, particularly on the [Kabul–Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul%E2%80%93Kandahar_Highway "Kabul–Kandahar Highway") and the [Kabul–Jalalabad Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul%E2%80%93Jalalabad_Road "Kabul–Jalalabad Road").[\[424\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-431) Traveling by bus in Afghanistan remains dangerous due to militant activities.[\[425\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-432)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Ariana_Afghan_A310-300_F-GEMO.jpg/220px-Ariana_Afghan_A310-300_F-GEMO.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ariana_Afghan_A310-300_F-GEMO.jpg)

An [Ariana Afghan Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariana_Afghan_Airlines "Ariana Afghan Airlines") [Airbus A310](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A310 "Airbus A310") in 2006

Air transport in Afghanistan is provided by the national carrier, [Ariana Afghan Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariana_Afghan_Airlines "Ariana Afghan Airlines"),[\[426\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-433) and by the private company [Kam Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kam_Air "Kam Air"). Airlines from a number of countries also provide flights in and out of the country. These include [Air India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_India "Air India"), [Emirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirates_\(airline\) "Emirates (airline)"), [Gulf Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_Air "Gulf Air"), [Iran Aseman Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_Aseman_Airlines "Iran Aseman Airlines"), [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines "Pakistan International Airlines"), and [Turkish Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Airlines "Turkish Airlines"). The country has four international airports: [Kabul International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_International_Airport "Kabul International Airport") (formerly Hamid Karzai International Airport), [Kandahar International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_International_Airport "Kandahar International Airport"), [Herat International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_International_Airport "Herat International Airport"), and [Mazar-e Sharif International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-e_Sharif_International_Airport "Mazar-e Sharif International Airport"). Including domestic airports, there are 43.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275) [Bagram Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagram_Air_Base "Bagram Air Base") is a major military airfield.

The country has three rail links: one, a 75-kilometre (47 mi) line from [Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-i-Sharif "Mazar-i-Sharif") to the [Uzbekistan border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Uzbekistan_Friendship_Bridge "Afghanistan–Uzbekistan Friendship Bridge");[\[427\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-434) a 10-kilometre (6.2 mi) long line from [Toraghundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toraghundi "Toraghundi") to the [Turkmenistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmenistan "Turkmenistan") border (where it continues as part of [Turkmen Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_Railways "Turkmen Railways")); and a short link from [Aqina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqina "Aqina") across the Turkmen border to [Kerki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerki "Kerki"), which is planned to be extended further across Afghanistan.[\[428\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-435) These lines are used for freight only and there is no passenger service. A rail line between [Khaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaf,_Iran "Khaf, Iran"), Iran and [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat"), western Afghanistan, intended for both freight and passengers, was under construction as of 2019.[\[429\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-436)[\[430\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-437) About 125 kilometres (78 mi) of the line will lie on the Afghan side.[\[431\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-438)[\[432\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-439)

Private vehicle ownership has increased substantially since the early 2000s. Taxis are yellow and consist of both cars and [auto rickshaws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auto_rickshaw "Auto rickshaw").[\[433\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-440) In rural Afghanistan, villagers often use donkeys, [mules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mule "Mule") or horses to transport or carry goods. Camels are primarily used by the Kochi nomads.[\[299\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Wahab-2007-306) Bicycles are popular throughout Afghanistan.[\[434\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-441)

## Demographics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Demographics")]

Main articles: [Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans "Afghans"), [Demographics of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Afghanistan "Demographics of Afghanistan"), and [Afghan diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_diaspora "Afghan diaspora")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/CIA_Afghanistan_ethnic_groups_map.jpg/290px-CIA_Afghanistan_ethnic_groups_map.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CIA_Afghanistan_ethnic_groups_map.jpg)

A Cold War-era CIA map showing traditional Afghan tribal territories. [Pashtun tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_tribes "Pashtun tribes") form the world's largest tribal society.[\[435\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-442)

### Population

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Population")]

The population of Afghanistan was estimated at 35.7 million as of 2024 by the Afghanistan [National Statistics and Information Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Statistics_and_Information_Authority "National Statistics and Information Authority"),[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NSIA_pop-23) whereas the UN estimates over 42.0 million.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-UN_pop-28) In 1979 the total population was reported to be about 15.5 million.[\[436\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-443) About 25.3% are [urbanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_area "Urban area"), 70.4% live in rural areas, and the remaining 4.3% are nomadic.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NSIA_pop-23) An additional 3 million or so Afghans are temporarily housed in neighboring [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Pakistan "Afghans in Pakistan") and [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Iran "Afghans in Iran"), most of whom were born and raised in those two countries. As of 2013, Afghanistan was the largest refugee-producing country in the world, a title held for 32 years.

The current population growth rate is 2.37%,[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275) one of the highest in the world outside of Africa. This population is expected to reach 82 million by 2050 if current population trends continue.[\[437\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-444) The population of Afghanistan increased steadily until the 1980s, when civil war caused millions to flee to other countries such as Pakistan.[\[438\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-445) Millions have since returned and the war conditions contribute to the country having the highest fertility rate outside Africa.[\[439\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-446) Afghanistan's healthcare has recovered since the turn of the century, causing falls in infant mortality and increases in life expectancy, although it has the lowest life expectance of any country outside Africa. This (along with other factors such as returning refugees) caused rapid population growth in the 2000s that has only recently started to slow down.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*] The [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient "Gini coefficient") in 2008 was 27.8.[\[440\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-447)

#### Fertility rate

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Fertility rate")]

Afghanistan 2024 [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate "Total fertility rate") has been estimated at 4.4.[\[441\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-448) In 2022 it was 4.5, about twice the world average rate.[\[442\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-449) The rate has fallen since the early 1980s.[\[443\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-450)

### Urbanization

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Urbanization")]

As estimated by the CIA World Factbook, 26% of the population was urbanized as of 2020. This is one of the lowest figures in the world; in Asia it is only higher than [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia "Cambodia"), [Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal "Nepal") and [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka "Sri Lanka"). Urbanization has increased rapidly, particularly in the capital [Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul "Kabul"), due to returning refugees from Pakistan and Iran after 2001, internally displaced people, and rural migrants.[\[444\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-451) Urbanization in Afghanistan is different from typical urbanization in that it is centered on just a few cities.[\[445\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aljazeera.com-452)

Kabul is the largest city, with a population of 5 million.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NSIA_pop-23) The other large cities are located generally in the "ring" around the Central Highlands, namely [Kandahar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar "Kandahar") in the south, [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") in the west, [Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-i-Sharif "Mazar-i-Sharif"), [Kunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz "Kunduz") in the north, and [Jalalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalalabad "Jalalabad") in the east.
