### Ethnicity and languages

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Ethnicity and languages")]

Main articles: [Ethnic groups in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Afghanistan "Ethnic groups in Afghanistan") and [Languages of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Afghanistan "Languages of Afghanistan")

See also: [Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans "Afghans")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/US_Army_ethnolinguistic_map_of_Afghanistan_--_circa_2001-09.jpg/290px-US_Army_ethnolinguistic_map_of_Afghanistan_--_circa_2001-09.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:US_Army_ethnolinguistic_map_of_Afghanistan_--_circa_2001-09.jpg)

Ethnolinguistic map of Afghanistan (2001)

[Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans "Afghans") are divided into several [ethnolinguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnolinguistic_group "Ethnolinguistic group") groups. According to research data by several institutions in 2019, the [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns "Pashtuns") are the largest ethnic group, comprising 42%, followed by [Tajiks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tajiks "Tajiks"), comprising 27%[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Population_Matters-4)[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-timesofindia-6)[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Population_2021-7)[\[447\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-454)[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-reliefweb.int-9) of the country's population. The other two major ethnic groups are the [Hazaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazaras "Hazaras") and [Uzbeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbeks "Uzbeks"), each at 9%. A further 10 other ethnic groups are recognized and each are represented in the [Afghan National Anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_National_Anthem "Afghan National Anthem").[\[448\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-455)

[Dari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dari "Dari") and [Pashto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto "Pashto") are the [official languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language "Official language") of Afghanistan; [bilingualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilingualism "Bilingualism") is very common.[\[449\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-456) Dari, which is also referred to as Eastern Persian as it is a variety of and mutually intelligible with [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_language "Persian language") (and very often called '[Farsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farsi "Farsi")' by some Afghans like in [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran")), functions as the [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca") in Kabul as well as in much of the northern and northwestern parts of the country.[\[450\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-457) Native speakers of Dari, of any ethnicity, are sometimes called [Farsiwans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farsiwan "Farsiwan").[\[451\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-458) Pashto is the native tongue of the [Pashtuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtuns "Pashtuns"), although many of them are also fluent in Dari while some non-Pashtuns are fluent in Pashto. Despite the Pashtuns having been dominant in Afghan politics for centuries, Dari remained the preferred language for government and bureaucracy.[\[452\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-459) According to [CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook "The World Factbook"), Dari Persian is spoken by 78% ([L1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language "First language") + [L2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_language "Second language")) and functions as the lingua franca, while [Pashto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto "Pashto") is spoken by 50%, [Uzbek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbek_language "Uzbek language") 10%, English 5%, [Turkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_language "Turkmen language") 2%, [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu") 2%, [Pashayi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashayi_languages "Pashayi languages") 1%, [Nuristani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuristani_languages "Nuristani languages") 1%, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic "Arabic") 1%, and [Balochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balochi_language "Balochi language") 1% (2021 est). Data represent the most widely spoken languages; shares sum to more than 100% because there is much bilingualism in the country and because respondents were allowed to select more than one language. There are a number of smaller regional languages, including Uzbek, Turkmen, Balochi, Pashayi, and Nuristani.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275)

When it comes to foreign languages among the populace, many are able to speak or understand [Hindustani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_language "Hindustani language") ([Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu "Urdu")-[Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi "Hindi")), partly due to returning [Afghan refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_Pakistan "Afghans in Pakistan") from [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan") and the popularity of [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood "Bollywood") films respectively.[\[453\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Afroz-2013-460) English is also understood by some of the population,[\[454\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-461) and has been gaining popularity as of the 2000s.[\[455\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-462) Some Afghans retain some ability in Russian, which was [taught in public schools during the 1980s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_occupation_of_Afghanistan "Russian occupation of Afghanistan").[\[453\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Afroz-2013-460)

### Religion

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Religion")]

Main article: [Religion in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Afghanistan "Religion in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Mazar-e_sharif_-_Steve_Evans.jpg/220px-Mazar-e_sharif_-_Steve_Evans.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mazar-e_sharif_-_Steve_Evans.jpg)

[Blue Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mosque_\(Mazar-i-Sharif\) "Blue Mosque (Mazar-i-Sharif)") in [Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-i-Sharif "Mazar-i-Sharif") is the largest mosque in Afghanistan.

The CIA estimated in 2009 that 99.7% of the Afghan population was Muslim[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275) and most are thought to adhere to the [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni "Sunni") [Hanafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi "Hanafi") school.[\[456\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-The_Swedish_Committee_for_Afghanistan_\(SCA\)-463) According to [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center"), as much as 90% are of the Sunni denomination, 7% [Shia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia "Shia") and 3% [non-denominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslim "Non-denominational Muslim").[\[457\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-464) The [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA "CIA") Factbook variously estimates up to 89.7% Sunni or up to 15% Shia.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275)

Afghan [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Afghanistan "Sikhism in Afghanistan") and [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Afghanistan "Hinduism in Afghanistan") are also found in certain major cities (namely Kabul, Jalalabad, Ghazni, Kandahar)[\[458\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-465)[\[459\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-466) accompanied by gurdwaras and mandirs.[\[460\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-467) According to [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle "Deutsche Welle") in September 2021, 250 remain in the country after 67 were evacuated to India.[\[461\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-468)

There was a small [Jewish community in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Afghanistan "History of the Jews in Afghanistan"), living mainly in Herat and Kabul. Over the years, this small community was forced to leave due to decades of warfare and religious persecution. By the end of the twentieth century, nearly the entire community had emigrated to Israel and the United States, with one known exception, Herat-born [Zablon Simintov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zablon_Simintov "Zablon Simintov"). He remained for years, being the caretaker of the only remaining Afghan synagogue. He left the country for the US after the second Taliban takeover. A woman who left shortly after him has since been identified as the likely last Jew in Afghanistan.[\[462\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-469)[\[463\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-470)[\[464\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-471)

[Afghan Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Christians "Afghan Christians"), who number 500–8,000, practice their faith secretly due to intense societal opposition, and there are no public churches.[\[465\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-472)[\[466\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-473)

### Education

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: Education")]

Main article: [Education in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Afghanistan "Education in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/UNESCO_Institute_of_Statistics_Afghanistan_Literacy_Rate_population_plus15_1980-2018.png/290px-UNESCO_Institute_of_Statistics_Afghanistan_Literacy_Rate_population_plus15_1980-2018.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:UNESCO_Institute_of_Statistics_Afghanistan_Literacy_Rate_population_plus15_1980-2018.png)

UNESCO Institute of Statistics Afghanistan Literacy Rate among population aged 15+ (1980–2018)

[Education in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Afghanistan "Education in Afghanistan") is overseen by the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Afghanistan\) "Ministry of Education (Afghanistan)") and the [Ministry of Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Higher_Education_\(Afghanistan\) "Ministry of Higher Education (Afghanistan)"). There are over 16,000 schools in the country and roughly 9 million students. Of this, about 60% are males and 40% females. However, the new regime has thus far forbidden female teachers and female students from returning to secondary schools.[\[467\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-474)[\[468\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-475) Over 174,000 students are enrolled in different [universities around the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Afghanistan "List of universities in Afghanistan"). About 21% of these are females.[\[469\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-476) Former Education Minister [Ghulam Farooq Wardak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulam_Farooq_Wardak "Ghulam Farooq Wardak") had stated that construction of 8,000 schools is required for the remaining children who are deprived of [formal learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_learning "Formal learning").[\[470\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-477) As of 2018 the literacy rate of the population age 15 and older is 43.02% (males 55.48% and females 29.81%).[\[471\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-478)

The top universities in Afghanistan are the [American University of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University_of_Afghanistan "American University of Afghanistan") (AUAF) followed by [Kabul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_University "Kabul University") (KU), both of which are located in Kabul. The [National Military Academy of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Military_Academy_of_Afghanistan "National Military Academy of Afghanistan"), modeled after the [United States Military Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Military_Academy "United States Military Academy") at West Point, was a four-year military development institution dedicated to graduating officers for the [Afghan Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Armed_Forces "Afghan Armed Forces"). The [Afghan Defense University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Defense_University "Afghan Defense University") was constructed near [Qargha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qargha "Qargha") in Kabul. Major universities outside of Kabul include [Kandahar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandahar_University "Kandahar University") in the south, [Herat University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat_University "Herat University") in the northwest, [Balkh University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh_University "Balkh University") and [Kunduz University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz_University "Kunduz University") in the north, [Nangarhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nangarhar_University "Nangarhar University") and [Khost University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khost_University "Khost University") in the east.

After the Taliban regained power in 2021, it became unclear to what extent female education would continue in the country. In March 2022, after they had been closed for some time, it was announced that secondary education would be reopened shortly. However, shortly before reopening, the order was rescinded and schools for older girls remained closed.[\[472\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-479) Despite the ban, six provinces, [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh_Province "Balkh Province"), [Kunduz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunduz_Province "Kunduz Province"), [Jowzjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jowzjan_Province "Jowzjan Province"), [Sar-I-Pul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sar-e_Pol_Province "Sar-e Pol Province"), [Faryab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faryab_Province "Faryab Province"), and the [Day Kundi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daykundi_Province "Daykundi Province"), still allow girl's schools from grade 6 and up.[\[473\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-480)[\[474\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-481) In December 2023, investigations were being held by the United Nations on the claim that Afghan girls of all ages were allowed to study at religious schools.[\[475\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-482) As of November 2024, some parts of the country allow women to attend religious schools to pursue dentistry, nursing, and other subjects.[\[476\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-483)

### Health

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=35 "Edit section: Health")]

Main article: [Health in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Afghanistan "Health in Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Kabul_Military_Hospital_-_panoramio.jpg/220px-Kabul_Military_Hospital_-_panoramio.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kabul_Military_Hospital_-_panoramio.jpg)

The [Daoud Khan Military Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daoud_Khan_Military_Hospital "Daoud Khan Military Hospital") in Kabul is one of the largest [hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Afghanistan "List of hospitals in Afghanistan") in Afghanistan.

According to the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index "Human Development Index"), Afghanistan is the [15th least developed country in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index "List of countries by Human Development Index"). The average [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy "List of countries by life expectancy") is estimated to be around 60 years.[\[477\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-484)[\[478\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-UNESCO-2016-485) The country's [maternal mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_mortality "Maternal mortality") rate is 396 deaths/100,000 live births and its [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality "Infant mortality") rate is 66[\[478\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-UNESCO-2016-485) to 112.8 deaths in every 1,000 live births.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275) The [Ministry of Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Public_Health_\(Afghanistan\) "Ministry of Public Health (Afghanistan)") plans to cut the infant mortality rate to 400 for every 100,000 live births before 2020. The country has more than 3,000 [midwives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwifery "Midwifery"), with an additional 300 to 400 being trained each year.[\[479\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-486)

There are over 100 [hospitals in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitals_in_Afghanistan "Hospitals in Afghanistan"),[\[480\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-487) with the most advanced treatments being available in Kabul. The [French Medical Institute for Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Medical_Institute_for_Children "French Medical Institute for Children") and [Indira Gandhi Children's Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi_Children%27s_Hospital "Indira Gandhi Children's Hospital") in Kabul are the leading [children's hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children%27s_hospital "Children's hospital") in the country. Some of the other leading hospitals in Kabul include the [Jamhuriat Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamhuriat_Hospital "Jamhuriat Hospital") and [Jinnah Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinnah_Hospital_\(Kabul\) "Jinnah Hospital (Kabul)").[\[481\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-488) In spite of all this, many Afghans travel to Pakistan and India for advanced treatment.

It was reported in 2006 that nearly 60% of the Afghan population lives within a two-hour walk of the nearest health facility.[\[482\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-489) The [disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability "Disability") rate is also high in Afghanistan due to the decades of war.[\[483\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-490) It was reported recently that about 80,000 people are missing limbs.[\[484\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-491)[\[485\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-492) Non-governmental charities such as [Save the Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Save_the_Children "Save the Children") and [Mahboba's Promise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahboba%27s_Promise "Mahboba's Promise") assist orphans in association with governmental structures.[\[486\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-493)

## Culture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=36 "Edit section: Culture")]

Main article: [Culture of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Afghanistan "Culture of Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/CH-NB_-_Afghanistan%2C_Kulm_%28Khulm%2C_Kholm%29-_Menschen_-_Annemarie_Schwarzenbach_-_SLA-Schwarzenbach-A-5-21-110.jpg/220px-CH-NB_-_Afghanistan%2C_Kulm_%28Khulm%2C_Kholm%29-_Menschen_-_Annemarie_Schwarzenbach_-_SLA-Schwarzenbach-A-5-21-110.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CH-NB_-_Afghanistan,_Kulm_\(Khulm,_Kholm\)-_Menschen_-_Annemarie_Schwarzenbach_-_SLA-Schwarzenbach-A-5-21-110.jpg)

An Afghan family near [Kholm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kholm,_Afghanistan "Kholm, Afghanistan"), 1939 – most Afghans are tribal.

[Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans "Afghans") have both common cultural features and those that differ between the regions of Afghanistan, each with distinctive cultures partly as a result of geographic obstacles that divide the country.[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fisher-2002-272) Family is the mainstay of Afghan society and families are often headed by a [patriarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch "Patriarch").[\[487\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-afghanistans.com-494) In the southern and eastern region, the people live according to the [Pashtun culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtun_culture "Pashtun culture") by following [Pashtunwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtunwali "Pashtunwali") (the Pashtun way).[\[488\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-495) Key tenets of Pashtunwali include [hospitality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melmastia "Melmastia"), the provision of [sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanawatai "Nanawatai") to those seeking refuge, and revenge for the shedding of blood.[\[489\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997126-496) The Pashtuns are largely connected to the culture of Central Asia and the [Iranian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Plateau "Iranian Plateau"). The remaining Afghans are culturally [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianization "Persianization") and [Turkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples "Turkic peoples"). Some non-Pashtuns who live in proximity with Pashtuns have adopted Pashtunwali in a process called [Pashtunization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashtunization "Pashtunization"), while some Pashtuns have been [Persianized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persianized "Persianized"). Those who have lived in Pakistan and Iran over the last 30 years have been further influenced by the cultures of those neighboring nations. The Afghan people are known to be strongly religious.[\[456\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-The_Swedish_Committee_for_Afghanistan_\(SCA\)-463)

Afghans, particularly Pashtuns, are noted for their tribal solidarity and high regard for personal honor.[\[490\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201259-497) There are various [Afghan tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Afghanistan "Ethnic groups in Afghanistan"), and an estimated 2–3 million [nomads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kochi_people "Kochi people").[\[491\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-498) Afghan culture is deeply [Islamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_culture "Islamic culture"),[\[492\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarfield201240%E2%80%9341-499) but pre-Islamic practices persist.[\[493\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997104-500) [Child marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage_in_Afghanistan "Child marriage in Afghanistan") is prevalent;[\[494\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-501) the legal age for marriage is 16.[\[495\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-502) The most preferred marriage in Afghan society is to one's [parallel cousin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_cousin "Parallel cousin"), and the groom is often expected to pay a [bride price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride_price "Bride price").[\[496\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997122,_198-503)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Kuchi_Village.jpg/220px-Kuchi_Village.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kuchi_Village.jpg)

A house occupied by nomadic *kochi* people in [Nangarhar Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nangarhar_Province "Nangarhar Province")

In the villages, families typically occupy [mudbrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudbrick "Mudbrick") houses, or compounds with mudbrick or [stone walled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_wall "Stone wall") houses. Villages typically have a headman (*malik*), a master for water distribution (*mirab*) and a religious teacher (*mullah*). Men would typically work on the fields, joined by women during harvest.[\[487\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-afghanistans.com-494) About 15% of the population are [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic "Nomadic"), locally called *[kochis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kochi_people "Kochi people")*.[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fisher-2002-272) When nomads pass villages they often buy supplies such as tea, wheat and [kerosene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerosene "Kerosene") from the villagers; villagers buy [wool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool "Wool") and milk from the nomads.[\[487\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-afghanistans.com-494)

[Afghan clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_clothing "Afghan clothing") for both men and women typically consists of various forms of [shalwar kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_kameez "Shalwar kameez"), especially *[perahan tunban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perahan_tunban "Perahan tunban")* and *[khet partug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khet_partug "Khet partug")*. Women would normally wear a *[chador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chador "Chador")* for head covering; some women, typically from highly conservative communities, wear the *[burqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burqa "Burqa")*, a full body covering. These were worn by some women of the Pashtun community well before Islam came to the region, but the [Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban") enforced this dress on women when they were in power.[\[497\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-504) Another popular dress is the *[chapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapan "Chapan")* which acts as a coat. The *[karakul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakul_\(hat\) "Karakul (hat)")* is a hat made from the fur of a specific regional breed of sheep. It was favored by former kings of Afghanistan and became known to much of the world in the 21st century when it was constantly worn by President [Hamid Karzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamid_Karzai "Hamid Karzai").[\[498\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-505) The *[pakol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakol "Pakol")* is another traditional hat originating from the far east of the country; it was popularly worn by the guerrilla leader [Ahmad Shah Massoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shah_Massoud "Ahmad Shah Massoud").[\[499\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-506) The *[Mazari hat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazari_hat "Mazari hat")* originates from northern Afghanistan.[\[500\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-507)

### Architecture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=37 "Edit section: Architecture")]

Main article: [Architecture of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Afghanistan "Architecture of Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Kabul_Skyline.jpg/260px-Kabul_Skyline.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kabul_Skyline.jpg)

Kabul skyline, displaying both historical and contemporary buildings

The nation has a complex history that has survived either in its current cultures or in the form of various languages and monuments. Afghanistan contains many remnants from all ages, including [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece "Ancient Greece") and [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist "Buddhist") stupas, monasteries, monuments, temples, and Islamic minarets. Among the most well known are the [Great Mosque of Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Herat "Great Mosque of Herat"), the [Blue Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mosque_\(Mazar-i-Sharif\) "Blue Mosque (Mazar-i-Sharif)"), the [Minaret of Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaret_of_Jam "Minaret of Jam"), the [Chil Zena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chil_Zena "Chil Zena"), the Qala-i Bost in [Lashkargah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lashkargah "Lashkargah"), the ancient Greek city of [Ai-Khanoum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ai-Khanoum "Ai-Khanoum").[\[501\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-508) However, many of its historic monuments have been damaged in modern times due to the civil wars.[\[502\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-509) The two famous [Buddhas of Bamiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhas_of_Bamiyan "Buddhas of Bamiyan") were destroyed by the Taliban, who regarded them as [idolatrous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatrous "Idolatrous"). As there was no colonialism in the modern era in Afghanistan, European-style architecture is rare but does exist: the Victory Arch at [Paghman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paghman "Paghman") and the [Darul Aman Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darul_Aman_Palace "Darul Aman Palace") in Kabul were built in this style in the 1920s. Afghan architecture also ranges [deep into India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Islamic_architecture "Indo-Islamic architecture") such as the city of [Agra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agra "Agra"),[\[503\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-510) and the [tomb of Sher Shah Suri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomb_of_Sher_Shah_Suri "Tomb of Sher Shah Suri"), an [Afghan Emperor of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sur_Empire "Sur Empire").[\[504\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-511)

### Art and ceramics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=38 "Edit section: Art and ceramics")]

Main article: [Afghan art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_art "Afghan art")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Traditional_Afghan_Embroidery_Style.jpg/220px-Traditional_Afghan_Embroidery_Style.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Traditional_Afghan_Embroidery_Style.jpg)

A traditional Afghan [embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embroidery "Embroidery") pattern

Carpet [weaving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weaving "Weaving") is an ancient practice in Afghanistan, and many of these are still [handmade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handicraft "Handicraft") by tribal and nomadic people today.[\[445\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aljazeera.com-452) Carpets have been produced in the region for thousands of years and traditionally done by women.[\[505\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-512) Some crafters express their feelings through the designs of rugs; for example after the outbreak of the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "Soviet–Afghan War"), "[war rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_rugs "War rugs")", a variant of [Afghan rugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_rug "Afghan rug"), were created with designs representing pain and misery caused by the conflict.[\[506\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-513) Every province has its own specific characteristics in making rugs.[\[507\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-514) In some of the Turkic-populated areas in the north-west, bride and wedding ceremony prices are driven by the bride's weaving skills.[\[508\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-515)

[Pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pottery "Pottery") has been crafted in Afghanistan for millennia. The village of [Istalif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istalif "Istalif"), north of Kabul, is in particular a major center, known for its unique turquoise and green pottery,[\[509\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-516) and their methods of crafting have remained the same for centuries.[\[510\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-517)[\[511\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-518) Much of *[lapis lazuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapis_lazuli "Lapis lazuli")* stones were earthed in modern-day Afghanistan which were used in [Chinese porcelain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_porcelain "Chinese porcelain") as [cobalt blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobalt_blue "Cobalt blue"), later used in ancient [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia "Mesopotamia") and Turkey.[\[512\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-519)

The lands of Afghanistan have a long history of art, with the world's earliest known usage of [oil painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_painting "Oil painting") found in cave murals in the country.[\[513\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-520)[\[514\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-521) A notable art style that developed in Afghanistan and eastern Pakistan is [Gandhara Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhara_Art "Gandhara Art"), produced by a fusion of [Greco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman "Greco-Roman") art and [Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_art "Buddhist art") between the 1st and 7th centuries CE.[\[515\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-522) Later eras saw increased use of the [Persian miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_miniature "Persian miniature") style, with [Kamaleddin Behzad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamaleddin_Behzad "Kamaleddin Behzad") of [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") being one of the most notable miniature artists of the [Timurid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_dynasty "Timurid dynasty") and early [Safavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid "Safavid") periods. Since the 1900s, the nation began to use Western techniques in art. [Abdul Ghafoor Breshna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Ghafoor_Breshna "Abdul Ghafoor Breshna") was a prominent Afghan painter and sketch artist from Kabul during the 20th century.

### Literature

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=39 "Edit section: Literature")]

See also: [Poetry of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetry_of_Afghanistan "Poetry of Afghanistan")

Classic [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature "Persian literature") and [Pashto poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto_poetry "Pashto poetry") are a cherished part of Afghan culture. Poetry has always been one of the major educational pillars in the region, to the level that it has integrated itself into culture.[\[516\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-523) One of the poetic styles is called [landay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landay_\(poetry\) "Landay (poetry)"). A popular theme in Afghan folklore and mythology are [Divs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dev_\(mythology\) "Dev (mythology)"), monstrous creatures.[\[517\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-524) Thursdays are traditionally "poetry night" in the city of [Herat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herat "Herat") when men, women and children gather and recite both ancient and modern poems.[\[518\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-525)

Three mystical authors are considered true national glories (although claimed with equal ardor by Iran), namely: [Khwaja Abdullah Ansari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khwaja_Abdullah_Ansari "Khwaja Abdullah Ansari") of Herat, a great mystic and [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi "Sufi") saint in the 11th century, [Sanai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanai "Sanai") of [Ghazni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazni "Ghazni"), author of mystical poems in the 12th century, and, finally, [Rumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi "Rumi") of [Balkh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkh "Balkh"), in the 13th century, considered the greatest mystical poet of the Muslim world. The Afghan Pashto literature, although quantitatively remarkable and in great growth in the last century, has always had an essentially local meaning and importance, feeling the influence of both Persian literature and the contiguous literatures of India. Both main literatures, from the second half of the nineteenth century, have shown themselves to be sensitive to genres, movements and stylistic features imported from Europe.

[Khushal Khan Khattak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khushal_Khan_Khattak "Khushal Khan Khattak") of the 17th century is considered the national poet. Other notable poets include [Rabi'a Balkhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabi%27a_Balkhi "Rabi'a Balkhi"), [Jami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jami "Jami"), [Rahman Baba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahman_Baba "Rahman Baba"), [Khalilullah Khalili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalilullah_Khalili "Khalilullah Khalili"), and [Parween Pazhwak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parween_Pazhwak "Parween Pazhwak").[\[519\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-526)

### Music

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=40 "Edit section: Music")]

Main article: [Music of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Afghanistan "Music of Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Afghan_rubab.jpg/220px-Afghan_rubab.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afghan_rubab.jpg)

The Afghan [rubab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubab_\(instrument\) "Rubab (instrument)")

Afghan classical music has close historical links with [Indian classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_classical_music "Indian classical music") and use the same Hindustani terminology and theories like [raga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga "Raga"). Genres of this style of music include [ghazal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazal "Ghazal") (poetic music) and instruments such as the Indian [tabla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabla "Tabla"), [sitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitar "Sitar") and [harmonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmonium "Harmonium"), and local instruments like [zerbaghali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zerbaghali "Zerbaghali"), as well as [dayereh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayereh "Dayereh") and [tanbur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanbur "Tanbur") which are also known in Central Asia, the Caucasus and the Middle East. The [rubab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubab_\(instrument\) "Rubab (instrument)") is the country's national instrument and precurses the Indian [sarod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarod "Sarod") instrument. Some of the famous artists of classical music include [Ustad Sarahang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ustad_Sarahang "Ustad Sarahang") and [Sarban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rahim_Sarban "Abdul Rahim Sarban").[\[520\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Songlines-527)

Pop music developed in the 1950s through [Radio Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Kabul "Radio Kabul") and was influential in social change. During this time female artists also started appearing, at first [Mermon Parwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mermon_Parwin "Mermon Parwin").[\[520\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Songlines-527) Perhaps the most famous artist of this genre was [Ahmad Zahir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Zahir "Ahmad Zahir"), who synthesized many genres and continues to be renowned for his voice and rich lyrics long after his death in 1979.[\[521\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-528)[\[520\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Songlines-527) Other notable masters of traditional or popular Afghan music include [Nashenas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashenas "Nashenas"), [Ubaidullah Jan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubaidullah_Jan "Ubaidullah Jan"), [Mahwash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahwash "Mahwash"), [Ahmad Wali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Wali "Ahmad Wali"), [Farhad Darya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farhad_Darya "Farhad Darya"), and [Naghma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naghma_\(singer\) "Naghma (singer)").[\[522\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-529)

[Attan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attan "Attan") is the national dance of Afghanistan, a group dance popularly performed by Afghans of all backgrounds.[\[523\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-530) The dance is considered part of Afghan identity.[\[524\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-531)

### Media and entertainment

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=41 "Edit section: Media and entertainment")]

Main article: [Media of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Afghanistan "Media of Afghanistan")

Afghanistan has around 350 [radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Afghanistan "List of radio stations in Afghanistan") and over 200 television stations.[\[525\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-TOLO-2019-532) [Radio Television Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Television_Afghanistan "Radio Television Afghanistan"), originating from 1925, is the state public broadcaster. Television programs began airing in the 1970s and today there are many private television channels such as [TOLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TOLO_\(TV_channel\) "TOLO (TV channel)") and [Shamshad TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamshad_TV "Shamshad TV"). The first Afghan newspaper was published in 1873,[\[526\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupree1997405-533) and there are hundreds of print outlets today.[\[525\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-TOLO-2019-532) By the 1920s, [Radio Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Kabul "Radio Kabul") was broadcasting local radio services.[\[527\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-534) [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America "Voice of America"), [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_World_Service "BBC World Service"), and [Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty "Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty") (RFE/RL) broadcast in both of Afghanistan's official languages on radio.[\[528\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-535) Press restrictions have been gradually relaxed and private media diversified since 2002, after more than two decades of tight controls.

Afghans have long been accustomed to watching Indian [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood "Bollywood") films and listening to its [filmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filmi "Filmi") songs.[\[529\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-536) It has been claimed that Afghanistan is among the biggest markets for the Hindi film industry.[\[530\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-537) The stereotypes of [Afghans in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans_in_India "Afghans in India") (*Kabuliwala* or *Pathani*) have also been represented in some Bollywood films by actors.[\[531\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-538) Many Bollywood film stars have roots in Afghanistan, including [Salman Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salman_Khan "Salman Khan"), [Saif Ali Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saif_Ali_Khan "Saif Ali Khan"), [Aamir Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aamir_Khan "Aamir Khan"), [Feroz Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feroz_Khan_\(Indian_actor\) "Feroz Khan (Indian actor)"), [Kader Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kader_Khan "Kader Khan"), [Naseeruddin Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naseeruddin_Shah "Naseeruddin Shah"), [Zarine Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarine_Khan "Zarine Khan"), [Celina Jaitly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celina_Jaitly "Celina Jaitly"), and a number of others. Several Bollywood films have been shot inside Afghanistan, including *[Dharmatma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharmatma "Dharmatma")*, *[Khuda Gawah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuda_Gawah "Khuda Gawah")*, *[Escape from Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escape_from_Taliban "Escape from Taliban")*, and *[Kabul Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabul_Express "Kabul Express")*.

### Cuisine

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=42 "Edit section: Cuisine")]

Main article: [Afghan cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_cuisine "Afghan cuisine")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Bread_of_Afghanistan_in_2010.jpg/220px-Bread_of_Afghanistan_in_2010.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bread_of_Afghanistan_in_2010.jpg)

[Non](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naan "Naan"), the most widely consumed bread in Afghanistan

Afghan cuisine is largely based upon the nation's chief crops, such as wheat, maize, [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley "Barley") and rice. Accompanying these staples are native fruits and vegetables as well as dairy products such as milk, [yogurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogurt "Yogurt"), and [whey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whey "Whey"). [Kabuli palaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabuli_palaw "Kabuli palaw") is the [national dish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_dish "National dish") of Afghanistan.[\[532\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-539) The nation's culinary specialties reflect its ethnic and geographic diversity.[\[533\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-540) Afghanistan is known for its high-quality [pomegranates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate_production_in_Afghanistan "Pomegranate production in Afghanistan"), grapes, and sweet melons.[\[534\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-541) Tea is a favorite drink among Afghans. A typical Afghan diet consists of [naan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naan "Naan"), yogurt, rice, and meat.[\[487\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-afghanistans.com-494)

### Holidays and festivals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=43 "Edit section: Holidays and festivals")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/%D9%87%D9%81%D8%AA_%D9%85%DB%8C%D9%88%D9%87.jpg/170px-%D9%87%D9%81%D8%AA_%D9%85%DB%8C%D9%88%D9%87.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%D9%87%D9%81%D8%AA_%D9%85%DB%8C%D9%88%D9%87.jpg)

Haft Mewa (Seven Fruit Syrup), popularly consumed during Nowruz

Afghanistan's official New Year starts with [Nowruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz "Nowruz"), an ancient tradition that started as a [Zoroastrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrian "Zoroastrian") celebration in present-day Iran, and with which it shares the annual celebration along with several other countries. It occurs every year at the [vernal equinox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_equinox "March equinox"). [In Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nauruz_in_Afghanistan "Nauruz in Afghanistan"), Nowruz is typically celebrated with music and dance, as well as holding [buzkashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzkashi "Buzkashi") tournaments.[\[535\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-542)

[Yaldā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yald%C4%81 "Yaldā"), another nationally celebrated ancient tradition,[\[536\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-543) commemorates the ancient goddess [Mithra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithra "Mithra") and marks the longest night of the year on the eve of the [winter solstice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_solstice "Winter solstice") (*čelle ye zemestān*; usually falling on 20 or 21 December),[\[537\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-544)[\[538\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-545) during which families gather together to recite poetry and eat fruit.[\[539\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-546)[\[540\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-547)

As a predominantly Muslim country, Islamic events and festivals such as [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan "Ramadan"), [Eid al-Fitr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eid_al-Fitr "Eid al-Fitr") and [Ashura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashura "Ashura") are widely celebrated annually in Afghanistan. The Sikh festival of [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi "Vaisakhi") is celebrated by the Sikh community[\[541\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-548) and the Hindu festival [Diwali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwali "Diwali") by the Hindu community.[\[542\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-549)

[National Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Independence_Day "Afghan Independence Day") is celebrated on 19 August to mark the [Anglo-Afghan Treaty of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Afghan_Treaty_of_1919 "Anglo-Afghan Treaty of 1919") and the country's full independence.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Factbook-275) Several international celebrations are also officially held in Afghanistan, such as [International Workers' Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Workers%27_Day "International Workers' Day"),[\[543\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-550) and [International Women's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Women%27s_Day "International Women's Day").[\[544\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-551) Some regional festivals include the [Red Flower Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nowruz#Afghanistan "Nowruz") (during Nowruz) in [Mazar-i-Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazar-i-Sharif "Mazar-i-Sharif"),[\[545\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-552) and the Damboora Festival in [Bamyan Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamyan_Province "Bamyan Province").[\[546\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-553)

### Sports

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=44 "Edit section: Sports")]

Main article: [Sport in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Afghanistan "Sport in Afghanistan")

See also: [Traditional games of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_of_Afghanistan "Traditional games of Afghanistan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Buzkashi_game_in_Afghanistan.jpg/220px-Buzkashi_game_in_Afghanistan.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Buzkashi_game_in_Afghanistan.jpg)

The ancient national sport of Afghanistan, [Buzkashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzkashi "Buzkashi")

Sport in Afghanistan is managed by the [Afghan Sports Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Sports_Federation "Afghan Sports Federation"). [Cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket "Cricket") and association football are the two most popular sports in the country.[\[547\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-554)[\[548\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-555) The Afghan Sports Federation promotes cricket, association football, basketball, volleyball, golf, [handball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_handball "Team handball"), [boxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing "Boxing"), [taekwondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taekwondo "Taekwondo"), [weightlifting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_weightlifting "Olympic weightlifting"), [bodybuilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodybuilding "Bodybuilding"), track and field, [skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_skating "Ice skating"), [bowling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowling "Bowling"), [snooker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snooker "Snooker"), [chess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chess "Chess"), and other sports.

The [Afghanistan national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_basketball_team "Afghanistan national basketball team") won the first team sports title at the [2010 South Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_South_Asian_Games "2010 South Asian Games").[\[549\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-556) In 2012, the country's [3x3 basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_3x3_team "Afghanistan national 3x3 team") won the gold medal at the [2012 Asian Beach Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3-on-3_basketball_at_the_2012_Asian_Beach_Games "3-on-3 basketball at the 2012 Asian Beach Games"). In 2013, Afghanistan's [football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_football_team "Afghanistan national football team") followed as it won the [SAFF Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAFF_Championship "SAFF Championship").[\[550\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-SAFF2013-557)

The [Afghan national cricket team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_national_cricket_team "Afghan national cricket team"), which was formed in 2001, won the [2009–10 ICC Intercontinental Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009%E2%80%9310_ICC_Intercontinental_Cup "2009–10 ICC Intercontinental Cup").[\[551\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-558) It won the [ACC Twenty20 Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Twenty20_Cup "ACC Twenty20 Cup") in 2007, 2009, 2011 and 2013. The team played in the [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Cricket_World_Cup "2015 Cricket World Cup"), [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Cricket_World_Cup "2019 Cricket World Cup"), and [2023 Cricket World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Cricket_World_Cup "2023 Cricket World Cup").[\[552\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-559) The [Afghanistan Cricket Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_Cricket_Board "Afghanistan Cricket Board") (ACB) is the official governing body of the sport and is based in Kabul. The [Alokozay Kabul International Cricket Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alokozay_Kabul_International_Cricket_Ground "Alokozay Kabul International Cricket Ground") serves as the nation's main cricket stadium. There are several other stadiums throughout the country, including the [Ghazi Amanullah Khan International Cricket Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Amanullah_Khan_International_Cricket_Stadium "Ghazi Amanullah Khan International Cricket Stadium") near [Jalalabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalalabad "Jalalabad"). Domestically, cricket is played between teams from different provinces.

The [Afghanistan national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_national_football_team "Afghanistan national football team") has been competing in international [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_football "Association football") since 1941.[\[553\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-560) The national team plays its home games at the [Ghazi Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazi_Stadium "Ghazi Stadium") in Kabul, while [football in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Afghanistan "Football in Afghanistan") is governed by the [Afghanistan Football Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan_Football_Federation "Afghanistan Football Federation"). The national team has never competed or qualified for the [FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_World_Cup "FIFA World Cup") but won an international football trophy in 2013.[\[550\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-SAFF2013-557) The country also has a national team in the sport of futsal, a 5-a-side variation of football.

The traditional and the national sport of Afghanistan is [buzkashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzkashi "Buzkashi"), particularly popular in the north.[\[554\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-561) It is similar to [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo "Polo"), played by horsemen in two teams, each trying to grab and hold a goat carcass.[\[555\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-562) The [Afghan Hound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Hound "Afghan Hound") (a type of running dog) originated in Afghanistan and was used in [wolf hunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_hunting_with_dogs "Wolf hunting with dogs").[\[556\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-563)

## Explanatory notes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit\&section=46 "Edit section: Explanatory notes")]

1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-5)** The last census in Afghanistan was conducted in 1979, and was itself incomplete. Due to the [ongoing conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_conflict "Afghan conflict") in the country, no official census has been conducted since.[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Population_Matters-4)
2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-Demonym_12-0)** Other demonyms that have been used are Afghani,[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10) Afghanese and Afghanistani (see [Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghans "Afghans") for further details)[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11)
3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-21)** Afghanistan is a pure [autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy "Autocracy"), with all law ultimately originating from the supreme leader. Consensus rule was initially used among the Taliban, but was phased out as the supreme leader monopolized control in the months following the 2021 return to power.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-17)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18)[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19) There is an advisory [Leadership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leadership_Council_of_Afghanistan "Leadership Council of Afghanistan"), however its role is in question as the supreme leader has not convened it for many months (as of March 2023),[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Afghanistan\&action=edit) and increasingly rules by decree.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20)
4. ^ [***a***](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-pop_note_31-0) [***b***](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-pop_note_31-1) The last census was conducted in 1979. Sources disagree about the current population:
   - The Afghani [National Statistics and Information Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Statistics_and_Information_Authority "National Statistics and Information Authority") gives an estimate of 35,695,527 for 2024.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NSIA_pop-23)[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24)
   - The [Encyclopædia Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Britannica "Encyclopædia Britannica") gives an estimate of 36,432,000 for 2025.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25)
   - The BBC gives a figure of 38.3 million for 2023.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-26)
   - The CIA gives an estimate of 40,121,552 for 2024.[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-27)
   - The UN gives an estimate of 42,045,000 for 2024.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-UN_pop-28)[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29)
   - The US Census Bureau provides an estimate of 49,552,566 for 2025.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30)
   All figures are mid-year.
5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-37)** [/æfˈɡænɪstæn, æfˈɡɑːnɪstɑːn/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/f/ff/En-us-Afghanistan.ogg/En-us-Afghanistan.ogg.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:En-us-Afghanistan.ogg "File:En-us-Afghanistan.ogg")
6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-38)**
   - [Pashto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashto_language "Pashto language"): د افغانستان اسلامي امارت
   - [Dari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dari_language "Dari language"): امارت اسلامی افغانستان
7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-40)** The [Government of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India "Government of India") regards Afghanistan as a bordering country, as it considers all of [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir "Kashmir") to be part of India. However, this is [disputed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_conflict "Kashmir conflict"), and the region bordering Afghanistan is administered by Pakistan as [Gilgit-Baltistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilgit-Baltistan "Gilgit-Baltistan").[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-39)

    - Cf: "Their name (Afghan) means "cavalier" being derived from the [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit "Sanskrit"), *Asva*, or *Asvaka*, a horse, and shows that their country must have been noted in ancient times, as it is at the present day, for its superior breed of horses. 147. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-154)** Klass, Rosanne (1994). [*The Widening Circle of Genocide*](https://books.google.com/books?id=I2chrSJCW54C\&pg=PA129). "During the intervening fourteen years of Communist rule, an estimated 1.5 to 2 million Afghan civilians were killed by Soviet forces and their proxies- the four Communist regimes in Kabul, and the East Germans, Bulgarians, Czechs, Cubans, Palestinians, Indians and others who assisted them. These were not battle casualties or the unavoidable civilian victims of warfare. Soviet and local Communist forces seldom attacked the scattered guerilla bands of the Afghan Resistance except, in a few strategic locales like the Panjsher valley. Instead they deliberately targeted the civilian population, primarily in the rural areas."

188. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-195)** ["Afghan Futures: A National Public Opinion Survey"](http://acsor-surveys.com/wp-content/uploads/2015/01/Afghan-Futures-Wave-6-Analysis_FINAL-v2.pdf) (PDF). *[Afghan Center for Socio-economic and Opinion Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Center_for_Socio-economic_and_Opinion_Research "Afghan Center for Socio-economic and Opinion Research")*. 29 January 2015. p. 4. Retrieved 2 January 2017. "Seventy-seven percent support the presence of U.S. forces; 67 percent say the same of NATO/ISAF forces more generally. Despite the country's travails, eight in 10 say it was a good thing for the United States to oust the Taliban in 2001. And much more blame either the Taliban or al Qaeda for the country's violence, 53 percent, than blame the United States, 12 percent. The latter is about half what it was in 2012, coinciding with a sharp reduction in the U.S. deployment."
